# Andrés Manuel López Obrador, Mexico, 2023

## 1. Overview

Andrés Manuel López Obrador (AMLO) was Mexico’s constitutional president and commander-in-chief throughout 2023. He exercised unusually strong agenda-setting influence through the Morena-led federal government, daily press conferences, control of the executive bureaucracy, and close relationships with the armed forces. His government also relied on the military for public security, infrastructure, customs, welfare administration, and major projects.

His influence was limited by Mexico’s federal structure, autonomous institutions, opposition parties, courts, the Supreme Court, the electoral authority (INE), state governors, organized crime, and practical dependence on military cooperation. In 2023, the Supreme Court invalidated López Obrador’s “Plan B” electoral reforms for serious legislative-procedure violations. Security policy remained a major failure area: homicide, disappearance, impunity, military accountability, and journalist safety remained severe problems. The government reported social and economic gains, but many 2023 social indicators were actually measurements of 2022 conditions.

## 2. Evidence records

### Record 1 — Military expansion into civilian security and state functions

- **Fact:** During 2023, López Obrador’s administration continued expanding the armed forces’ role in public security and civilian administration. The National Guard’s operational and administrative control had been transferred to the Defense Ministry, while the Army and Navy performed extensive civilian tasks. The Inter-American Commission on Human Rights concluded that this represented backsliding from the planned withdrawal of the armed forces from public security.
- **Why it matters:** This was a central institutional choice of the president’s security model and increased military influence over domestic governance.
- **Strongest source:** *Annual Report 2023, Chapter V: Mexico*, Inter-American Commission on Human Rights, 2024, pp. 3–5 and 104–105. [Direct source](https://www.oas.org/en/iachr/docs/annual/2023/chapters/IA2023_Cap_5_Mexico_ENG.PDF)
- **Locator:** PDF pp. 3–5, paras. 7–13; PDF pp. 104–105, para. 438.
- **Period:** 2023, with inherited institutional developments from 2019–2022.
- **Ruler connection:** Strong. The president promoted and defended the military-centered model.
- **Complicating evidence:** The government reported that the National Guard and military deployment improved state capacity and public security coverage; the IACHR also recorded state claims of a gradual withdrawal plan.
- **Cautions:** The IACHR report combines state submissions, civil-society information, and the Commission’s own assessment; it does not independently measure every operational outcome.
- **Independent support:** Human Rights Watch, *Ayotzinapa, Mexico’s Army, and López Obrador’s Silence* (2023); Human Rights Watch, *World Report 2024: Mexico*.

### Record 2 — Persistent homicide and public-safety crisis

- **Fact:** Mexico recorded 15,082 homicides during January–June 2023, a preliminary national rate of 12 per 100,000 inhabitants; firearms were involved in 71.3 percent of cases. The first-half rate was lower than in 2022 but remained extremely high.
- **Why it matters:** It provides a reliable national measure of the violence environment during the requested period and complicates claims of a decisive security turnaround.
- **Strongest source:** *Deaths due to homicide from January to June 2023 (preliminary)*, INEGI, January 23, 2024. [Direct source](https://en.www.inegi.org.mx/app/saladeprensa/noticia/8768)
- **Locator:** Press-release summary and preliminary first-semester homicide statistics.
- **Period:** 2023.
- **Ruler connection:** Moderate to strong. Homicides are affected by state and municipal authorities and organized crime, but federal security policy was a major component of the administration’s program.
- **Complicating evidence:** The rate declined from the comparable 2022 period; homicide attribution to the president is necessarily indirect.
- **Cautions:** These are registered deaths, not necessarily all violent deaths, and the data were preliminary.
- **Independent support:** IACHR, *Annual Report 2023, Chapter V: Mexico*; U.S. Department of State, *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Mexico*.

### Record 3 — Ayotzinapa investigation stalled amid military resistance

- **Fact:** López Obrador created a truth commission and renewed the official investigation into the 2014 disappearance of 43 Ayotzinapa students, but by 2023 the investigation had stalled amid allegations that military institutions withheld evidence and that authorities protected senior military personnel.
- **Why it matters:** It tests the president’s stated anti-impunity and truth-seeking commitments against his willingness to confront the armed forces.
- **Strongest source:** *Ayotzinapa, Mexico’s Army, and López Obrador’s Silence*, Human Rights Watch, September 26, 2023. [Direct source](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/09/26/ayotzinapa-mexicos-army-and-lopez-obradors-silence)
- **Locator:** Paras. 641–659, especially 650–657.
- **Period:** 2023, concerning an inherited 2014 atrocity and a 2018–2023 investigation.
- **Ruler connection:** Strong. The president created the investigative framework and had authority to demand cooperation from federal institutions.
- **Complicating evidence:** His administration did produce a new official report, arrest warrants, and a truth commission; the original disappearance and much of the obstruction predated his presidency.
- **Cautions:** HRW is an advocacy organization and relies partly on investigative reporting and expert accounts; some allegations remain contested.
- **Independent support:** Interdisciplinary Group of Independent Experts (GIEI) reports; Associated Press reporting on military obstruction; IACHR materials on disappearances.

### Record 4 — Pegasus surveillance allegations involving journalists and officials

- **Fact:** In 2023, investigative organizations published evidence that a secret military intelligence unit used Pegasus spyware against human-rights defender Raymundo Ramos; reporting also stated that presidential ally and human-rights official Alejandro Encinas had been targeted. López Obrador denied that his government used Pegasus for unlawful spying.
- **Why it matters:** The allegation directly concerns executive accountability, military autonomy, privacy, press freedom, and the government’s credibility.
- **Strongest source:** *Mexico: Freedom on the Net 2023 Country Report*, Freedom House, 2023. [Direct source](https://freedomhouse.org/country/mexico/freedom-net/2023)
- **Locator:** Paras. 414–419.
- **Period:** 2023, with surveillance activity partly occurring in 2019–2022.
- **Ruler connection:** Strong institutionally, but personal authorization by López Obrador was not established in the source.
- **Complicating evidence:** The president denied the allegations and argued that military intelligence was not equivalent to civilian spying.
- **Cautions:** Freedom House summarizes investigations rather than serving as the forensic source itself; the precise chain of authorization remains unresolved.
- **Independent support:** Citizen Lab forensic analysis; R3D, Article 19, and SocialTIC’s “Ejército Espía” investigation; New York Times reporting on Encinas.

### Record 5 — Supreme Court invalidated the “Plan B” electoral reform

- **Fact:** On June 22, 2023, Mexico’s Supreme Court invalidated the second part of López Obrador’s electoral reform package by a 9–2 vote, finding serious violations of legislative procedure, including insufficient notice, rushed consideration, and improper changes between chambers.
- **Why it matters:** It demonstrates a concrete institutional limit on presidential and congressional power and preserved the pre-reform legal framework governing the INE and elections.
- **Strongest source:** *Invalida la Corte la segunda parte del paquete de reformas político-electorales 2022–2023 por violaciones al procedimiento legislativo*, Supreme Court of Justice of the Nation, June 22, 2023. [Direct source](https://www.internet2.scjn.gob.mx/red2/comunicados/noticia.asp?id=7408)
- **Locator:** Press release No. 222/2023; listed procedural violations and effects of invalidation.
- **Period:** 2023.
- **Ruler connection:** Strong. The reforms were proposed and politically sponsored by the president and his congressional coalition.
- **Complicating evidence:** The administration argued that the reform reduced excessive electoral bureaucracy and spending; the Court’s ruling was based primarily on procedure rather than the entire policy substance.
- **Cautions:** The press release is an official summary; the full judgment is the authoritative legal document.
- **Independent support:** Electoral Tribunal rulings; reporting by Al Jazeera and *El País*; INE legal filings.

### Record 6 — Poverty and social-policy evidence improved, but 2023 data were lagged

- **Fact:** Mexico’s official multidimensional poverty results released in 2023 were based on the 2022 ENIGH survey, not conditions measured during 2023. CONEVAL presented the results as a national and state-level assessment of income poverty and social deprivations.
- **Why it matters:** It prevents researchers from treating 2023 poverty data as a direct contemporaneous evaluation of López Obrador’s final full year of policy implementation.
- **Strongest source:** *Pobreza en México: Resultados de pobreza en México 2022*, CONEVAL, released using ENIGH 2022 data published July 26, 2023. [Direct source](https://www.coneval.org.mx/Medicion/Paginas/PobrezaInicio.aspx)
- **Locator:** Paras. 77–93 of the webpage.
- **Period:** 2023 publication; underlying conditions measured in 2022.
- **Ruler connection:** Moderate. Federal social policy may affect outcomes, but poverty is also shaped by labor markets, remittances, inflation, state policy, and post-pandemic recovery.
- **Complicating evidence:** CONEVAL’s methodology is constitutionally mandated and transparent; improvements in income and labor conditions may still reflect policies implemented before the survey.
- **Cautions:** The source cannot establish 2023 causal effects.
- **Independent support:** CONEVAL, *Informe de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social 2022*; World Bank and OECD macroeconomic assessments.

### Record 7 — Protection mechanisms for journalists existed but remained inadequate

- **Fact:** In 2023, the Mexican government reported increased funding and staffing for the Protection Mechanism for Human Rights Defenders and Journalists. Civil-society organizations nevertheless reported continued violence, stigmatization by authorities, weak implementation, and inadequate participation in the proposed legal reforms.
- **Why it matters:** It captures both an administrative effort and the persistent failure to provide effective press and defender protection.
- **Strongest source:** *Annual Report 2023, Chapter V: Mexico*, IACHR, 2024. [Direct source](https://www.oas.org/en/iachr/docs/annual/2023/chapters/IA2023_Cap_5_Mexico_ENG.PDF)
- **Locator:** PDF pp. 49–50, paras. 196–197; pp. 91–92, paras. 380–382; pp. 104–105, para. 438.
- **Period:** 2023.
- **Ruler connection:** Strong at the federal policy level; protection also depends on state authorities and local prosecutors.
- **Complicating evidence:** The state reported 569 million pesos budgeted for 2023, more than 1.8 billion pesos allocated during the administration, and a 70 percent increase in mechanism staff.
- **Cautions:** The IACHR records competing state and civil-society submissions; journalist-killing totals require separate verification from specialized databases.
- **Independent support:** Article 19’s 2023 Mexico reports; Committee to Protect Journalists; Freedom House.

### Record 8 — Information quality was constrained by institutional distrust and conflicting narratives

- **Fact:** Reliable evaluation was difficult because official agencies, independent journalists, human-rights organizations, and the president’s office often presented conflicting accounts of military conduct, disappearances, surveillance, homicide, and disaster response. International bodies repeatedly identified incomplete registries, weak coordination, insufficient investigation, and stigmatization of critics.
- **Why it matters:** This affects confidence in causal attribution and requires separating official outputs from independent verification.
- **Strongest source:** *Annual Report 2023, Chapter V: Mexico*, IACHR, 2024. [Direct source](https://www.oas.org/en/iachr/docs/annual/2023/chapters/IA2023_Cap_5_Mexico_ENG.PDF)
- **Locator:** PDF pp. 104–105, para. 438; PDF pp. 91–92, paras. 380–382.
- **Period:** 2023, with institutional weaknesses inherited from earlier years.
- **Ruler connection:** Moderate to strong. The administration controlled official communication and frequently challenged independent reporting, while many data systems were shared across federal and state institutions.
- **Complicating evidence:** Mexico retained active statistical agencies, courts, international monitoring, investigative journalism, and formal transparency mechanisms.
- **Cautions:** “Information environment” is a synthesis rather than a single numerical indicator.
- **Independent support:** Freedom House, *Freedom in the World 2023* and *Freedom on the Net 2023*; U.S. State Department human-rights report; OHCHR Committee on Enforced Disappearances.

## 3. Later retrospective evidence

Later sources are useful for assessing outcomes but should not be mistaken for contemporaneous 2023 evidence.

- Human Rights Watch’s *World Report 2024: Mexico* states that the officially registered number of disappeared persons exceeded 110,000 in 2023 and reports continuing evidence of military Pegasus surveillance. [Source](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/mexico)
- The IACHR’s 2023 annual report, published later, retrospectively assessed Mexico’s 2023 compliance and concluded that militarization, disappearances, impunity, and attacks on journalists remained serious concerns.
- Later economic assessments by the OECD and World Bank can help evaluate post-2023 growth, wages, poverty, and nearshoring, but should be kept separate from evidence available during the period.

## 4. Information environment and evidence limitations

The strongest evidence came from INEGI, CONEVAL, the Supreme Court, the IACHR, OHCHR, and forensic surveillance investigations. The main limitations were:

- homicide, disappearance, and human-rights data were distributed across federal and state systems with inconsistent reporting;
- many social indicators released in 2023 measured 2022;
- military institutions controlled or withheld information relevant to Ayotzinapa, surveillance, and security operations;
- journalists and defenders faced intimidation, violence, surveillance, and public stigmatization;
- López Obrador’s daily conferences provided extensive primary statements but also produced highly polarized official-versus-independent narratives;
- attribution is difficult because organized crime, state governments, prosecutors, police, courts, and the federal executive all shared responsibility.

## 5. Questions for deeper research

1. Did homicide and disappearance trends improve in areas where the National Guard expanded, compared with areas relying mainly on civilian police?
2. What documentary evidence links the federal executive to Pegasus procurement, authorization, or knowledge?
3. Which Ayotzinapa documents did the military withhold, and what did independent experts conclude they would likely establish?
4. How did military spending and civilian-task expansion affect public finances and institutional accountability?
5. What were the precise 2023 effects of wage increases, welfare transfers, remittances, and inflation on household living standards?
6. How many journalists and defenders were killed, disappeared, threatened, or protected during 2023, using a reconciled database?
7. Did the Supreme Court’s electoral rulings materially alter the 2024 electoral environment?
8. Which federal programs achieved measurable improvements in health, education, infrastructure, and essential services during 2023?

## 6. Corroborating sources opened and useful mainly for support

- [U.S. Department of State, 2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Mexico](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107784.html)
- [Human Rights Watch, World Report 2023: Mexico](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2023/country-chapters/mexico)
- [Freedom House, Mexico: Freedom in the World 2023](https://freedomhouse.org/country/mexico/freedom-world/2023)
- [OHCHR Committee on Enforced Disappearances, 2023 materials concerning Mexico](https://docstore.ohchr.org/SelfServices/FilesHandler.ashx?enc=FPPuOXroO0GpxG%2FTAa3Co7%2Bo%2FxWB%2F9oaG8DVBhFD3yEi%2BUbSnWq23oB8HM1%2FFfiqWpq3nu7%2FX7vfGDHjaOYeww%3D%3D)
- [CONEVAL, Social Development Policy Evaluation Report 2022](https://www.coneval.org.mx/InformesPublicaciones/Paginas/Mosaicos/IEPDS_2022.aspx)
- [INEGI, preliminary homicide statistics for January–June 2023](https://en.www.inegi.org.mx/app/saladeprensa/noticia/8768)
- [Supreme Court of Mexico, Plan B invalidation communiqué](https://www.internet2.scjn.gob.mx/red2/comunicados/noticia.asp?id=7408)

## 7. Promising leads still needing inspection

- Full Supreme Court judgment for constitutional actions 71/2023 and related cases.
- Full GIEI Ayotzinapa final report and underlying document requests.
- Citizen Lab’s forensic report on Pegasus targeting of Raymundo Ramos.
- New York Times investigation concerning Alejandro Encinas.
- 2023 reports from Article 19, CPJ, Reporters Without Borders, and the Mexican Protection Mechanism.
- INEGI’s full-year 2023 homicide release and state-level tables.
- CONEVAL’s 2022 statistical annex and program-level evaluations.
- Official 2023 budget and procurement records for the National Guard, SEDENA, and military-operated infrastructure.
- 2023 education, health, labor, and welfare administrative data with direct federal-program attribution.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 1B ---

# Chapter 1B — Nuclear and Existential-Risk Responsibility

Period: 2023–2023  
Ruler: Andrés Manuel López Obrador, President of Mexico  
Prompt version: `chapter_research_prompt_v2`  
Lens presentation: `layered_lenses_v1`

## 1. Orientation

López Obrador held Mexico’s formal executive authority and was commander-in-chief. Nuclear-weapons decisions were nevertheless constrained by Mexico’s treaty obligations, the Foreign Ministry, the National Commission for Nuclear Safety and Safeguards (CNSNS), the state electricity utility CFE, the IAEA safeguards system, and international nonproliferation institutions.

Mexico did not possess a nuclear-weapons arsenal identified in the principal public arms-control sources examined. Its nuclear exposure was instead concentrated in:

- one civilian nuclear power station, Laguna Verde, with two reactors;
- radioactive-source security and transport;
- safeguards and export controls;
- biological and chemical dual-use materials;
- cyber risks affecting state and critical infrastructure;
- diplomatic management of global nuclear escalation.

The evidence indicates that Mexico inherited a strongly non-nuclear posture and maintained it in 2023. López Obrador’s government also used Mexico’s diplomatic position to advocate nuclear disarmament. However, the record is stronger for treaty adherence and diplomatic rhetoric than for evidence of new domestic safeguards, independent expert oversight, or a measurable improvement in Mexico’s long-term catastrophic-risk posture.

## 2. Disposition by lens

| Lens | Disposition | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| 1B.1 Reducing existential risk | Evidence developed | WEB-1B-002, WEB-1B-003, WEB-1B-006 |
| 1B.2 Responsible nuclear rhetoric | Evidence developed | WEB-1B-003 |
| 1B.3 Safe nuclear control | Evidence developed, mixed | WEB-1B-004, WEB-1B-005 |
| 1B.4 Arms control and inspections | Evidence developed | WEB-1B-001, WEB-1B-002 |
| 1B.5 Nuclear cover for aggression | No evidence of Mexican nuclear capability or nuclear coercion | WEB-1B-001, WEB-1B-003 |
| 1B.6 Preventing proliferation | Evidence developed, but mostly inherited and institutional | WEB-1B-001, WEB-1B-006 |
| 1B.7 Independent risk expertise | Partial evidence only | WEB-1B-004, WEB-1B-006 |
| 1B.8 Crisis de-escalation | Evidence developed at diplomatic level; no nuclear crisis involving Mexico located | WEB-1B-003 |
| 1B.9 Other catastrophic risks | Partial evidence: cyber and biological-security activity; limited outcome evidence | WEB-1B-006 |

## 3. Compact evidence index

### WEB-1B-001 — Mexico’s inherited treaty-based nonproliferation posture

**Claim and significance:** Mexico maintained a formal non-nuclear posture through the Treaty of Tlatelolco, the NPT, and the TPNW. The IAEA country record lists Mexico’s safeguards arrangements and Additional Protocol. This substantially restricted any Mexican move toward nuclear weapons and provided external verification mechanisms.

**Strongest source:** *Country List: Mexico*, IAEA Office of Legal Affairs, current treaty-status record. [Direct source](https://ola.iaea.org/Applications/FactSheets/Country/Detail?code=MX)

**Locator:** Mexico entry listing the safeguards agreement, Additional Protocol, Convention on Nuclear Safety, and related instruments.

**Period status:** Inherited baseline, continuing in 2023.

**Ruler attribution:** Weak to moderate. The legal posture predates López Obrador, although his government had authority to comply with or weaken it.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** Treaty membership does not by itself prove effective implementation, comprehensive domestic controls, or absence of undeclared activity. Public sources did not establish a 2023 Mexican nuclear-weapons program.

**Source cautions:** The IAEA treaty list establishes legal status, not the quality of every domestic safeguard or inspection.

**Independent support:** UNODA Treaty of Tlatelolco page; SIPRI nuclear-weapons inventory, which identifies the nine nuclear-armed states and does not identify Mexico as one of them. [SIPRI](https://www.sipri.org/yearbook/2023/07)

**Relevant lenses:** 1B.1, 1B.3, 1B.4, 1B.5, 1B.6, 1B.10.

---

### WEB-1B-002 — Mexico presided over the 2023 TPNW meeting

**Claim and significance:** Mexico presided over the Second Meeting of States Parties to the Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons in November–December 2023. The meeting evaluated implementation and included discussion of the humanitarian consequences of nuclear weapons. This was an active diplomatic contribution to nuclear-risk reduction rather than merely passive treaty membership.

**Strongest source:** *Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons — Second Meeting of States Parties (2023)*, United Nations Office for Disarmament Affairs. [Direct source](https://meetings.unoda.org/tpnw/tpnw-msp-2023)

**Locator:** Meeting overview identifying Mexico’s Juan Ramón de la Fuente as president; meeting documentation for the November 27–December 1, 2023 session.

**Period status:** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution:** Moderate. The meeting was conducted by Mexico’s diplomatic apparatus during López Obrador’s presidency, although the chair was the Mexican ambassador rather than the president personally.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** Presiding over a diplomatic meeting does not demonstrate that nuclear-armed states changed their arsenals or doctrines. The TPNW remained politically contested by nuclear-armed states and their allies.

**Source cautions:** UNODA documents establish formal participation and agenda, not the meeting’s ultimate policy impact.

**Independent support:** Mexican Foreign Ministry communiqué on the meeting; UNODA 2023 Yearbook. [Mexican Foreign Ministry](https://www.gob.mx/sre/prensa/concluye-con-exito-la-segunda-reunion-de-las-partes-del-tratado-sobre-la-prohibicion-de-armas-nucleares-presidida-por-mexico)

**Relevant lenses:** 1B.1, 1B.2, 1B.4, 1B.8.

---

### WEB-1B-003 — Mexico advocated nuclear disarmament and condemned nuclear threats

**Claim and significance:** In an October 4, 2023 statement to the UN First Committee, Mexico spoke for the New Agenda Coalition. The statement condemned increasingly threatening nuclear rhetoric connected to the war in Ukraine, called for verifiable and irreversible disarmament, supported the TPNW, and rejected threats to use nuclear weapons.

**Strongest source:** *Statement by Mexico on behalf of the New Agenda Coalition at the First Committee: General Debate*, Government of Mexico / UNODA, October 4, 2023. [Direct source](https://docs-library.unoda.org/General_Assembly_First_Committee_-Seventy-Eighth_session_%282023%29/Statement_by_Mexico_on_behalf_of_the_New_Agenda_Coalition_at_en.pdf)

**Locator:** PDF pp. 1–3, especially paras. 24–39, 48–83, and 101–111.

**Period status:** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution:** Moderate. The statement represented Mexico’s official foreign policy during López Obrador’s presidency. Direct personal authorship by López Obrador was not established.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** Mexico’s diplomatic rhetoric did not produce direct control over the nuclear policies of Russia, the United States, China, or other nuclear-armed states. Mexico’s broader policy of military expansion domestically does not itself demonstrate nuclear irresponsibility.

**Source cautions:** This is an official diplomatic statement and therefore strong evidence of formal rhetoric, but not of implementation or measurable outcomes.

**Independent support:** UNODA 2023 Yearbook; UN record of the TPNW Second Meeting of States Parties.

**Relevant lenses:** 1B.1, 1B.2, 1B.4, 1B.8, 1B.10.

---

### WEB-1B-004 — Mexico retained civilian nuclear infrastructure and formal safety institutions

**Claim and significance:** Mexico operated the Laguna Verde nuclear power station, its only nuclear power plant, and maintained the CNSNS as the national nuclear-safety and safeguards authority. The IAEA’s nuclear-power profile identifies two operating reactors with a combined capacity of approximately 1,620 MW.

**Strongest source:** *Country Nuclear Power Profiles: Mexico*, IAEA. [Direct source](https://cnpp.iaea.org/public/countries/MX/profile/preview)

**Locator:** Mexico profile identifying Laguna Verde, two operating units, and installed capacity.

**Period status:** Target-year institutional context, with the plant inherited from earlier administrations.

**Ruler attribution:** Weak to moderate. The plant and regulator predated López Obrador, though the federal executive controlled the responsible ministries and state utility.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** A functioning civilian nuclear sector and IAEA-linked safeguards are positive institutional controls, but public profile data do not establish the adequacy of staffing, maintenance budgets, emergency preparedness, or regulator independence during 2023.

**Source cautions:** The profile is primarily descriptive and does not constitute a 2023 safety evaluation.

**Independent support:** IAEA country legal record; Mexican ININ information on nuclear safety. [ININ](https://www.gob.mx/inin/es/articulos/la-importancia-de-la-seguridad-en-centrales-nucleares?idiom=es)

**Relevant lenses:** 1B.3, 1B.4, 1B.7, 1B.10.

---

### WEB-1B-005 — Theft of a radioactive source exposed a real 2023 radiological-security risk

**Claim and significance:** On October 18, 2023, an industrial radiography camera containing a Category 3 Iridium-192 source was stolen in Ciudad del Carmen, Tabasco, along with its vehicle. The IAEA recorded no reported public or worker overexposure and no release beyond authorized limits, but classified the incident as INES level 2 and recorded degradation of defense-in-depth controls.

**Strongest source:** *Stolen cat-3 source in Mexico*, IAEA Incident and Emergency Centre, October 18, 2023. [Direct source](https://www-news.iaea.org/ErfView.aspx?mId=5a43e799-5249-49dd-b649-c5ac897a1dab)

**Locator:** Event record, lines identifying the date, location, Ir-192 source, provisional INES rating, and “degradation of defence in-depth.”

**Period status:** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution:** Moderate institutionally, weak personally. The incident occurred under federal regulatory jurisdiction, but the source was privately or industrially used and the record does not establish presidential involvement.

**Contrary or mitigating evidence:** The IAEA record states that there was no release, injury, or overexposure and that there was no continuing problem. The source was not a nuclear weapon or weapons-usable material.

**Source cautions:** IAEA incident reports are based partly on information supplied by national authorities, which remain responsible for the content. The report does not evaluate the causes of the theft or subsequent corrective action.

**Independent support:** CNSNS contact information in the IAEA record; further Mexican regulatory and prosecution records should be obtained.

**Relevant lenses:** 1B.3, 1B.6, 1B.10.

---

### WEB-1B-006 — 2023 biological-security and cyber initiatives were mainly preventive and lacked outcome evidence

**Claim and significance:** In September 2023, Mexico hosted an exchange with Brazil and Chile on implementation of the Biological Weapons Convention and UN Security Council Resolution 1540, involving customs, financial intelligence, health, agriculture, economy, communications, and defense bodies. Separately, the National Guard held a 2023 cybersecurity program involving federal cyber, defense, intelligence, private-sector, and civil-society participants. These activities show institutional attention to dual-use and cyber risks, but the sources do not demonstrate measurable risk reduction.

**Strongest source:** *Mexico exchanges information and best practices with Brazil and Chile to strengthen cooperation on terrorism and biological weapons*, Mexican Foreign Ministry, September 28, 2023. [Direct source](https://www.gob.mx/sre/prensa/mexico-exchanges-information-and-best-practices-with-brazil-and-chile-to-strengthen-cooperation-on-terrorism-and-biological-weapons?idiom=en)

**Locator:** Press-release description of the participating agencies and the BWC/Resolution 1540 focus.

**Corroborating source:** *Guardia Nacional inaugura Jornada de Ciberseguridad 2023*, National Guard, April 12, 2023. [Direct source](https://www.gob.mx/guardianacional/prensa/guardia-nacional-inaugura-jornada-de-ciberseguridad-2023-internet-seguro-para-todas-y-todos)

**Locator:** Paras. 49–77, especially the stated objectives, participating agencies, and cyber-risk topics.

**Period status:** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution:** Moderate. These were federal-government initiatives during López Obrador’s administration, but no evidence shows that he personally designed or directed them.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** The initiatives may represent useful coordination and prevention. However, the public records provide no budgets, staffing totals, inspection results, incident reductions, or independent evaluations.

**Source cautions:** Both are official government communications and are therefore self-descriptive; one Foreign Ministry page was accessible mainly through search metadata, while the National Guard page was fully opened.

**Independent support:** UNODA Biological Weapons Convention confidence-building-measures materials; UN Security Council Resolution 1540 documentation.

**Relevant lenses:** 1B.1, 1B.6, 1B.7, 1B.9.

## 4. Information environment and limits

The nuclear record was easier to verify than the domestic-security record because treaty status, UN proceedings, IAEA registers, and diplomatic statements are public and relatively stable. The main limitations were:

- Mexico’s non-nuclear status is easier to establish than the quality of domestic implementation.
- Public sources do not provide a complete 2023 budget and staffing audit for CNSNS, Laguna Verde, radioactive-source security, or biological export controls.
- Official statements emphasize legal compliance and cooperation but rarely provide independent outcome measures.
- The 2023 radioactive-source incident demonstrates that radiological security risks existed, but public records do not reveal whether the problem resulted from regulatory weakness, criminal theft, operator failure, or a combination.
- No credible evidence was located of Mexican nuclear threats, nuclear coercion, nuclear aggression, or a nuclear crisis directly involving López Obrador.
- Cybersecurity and biological-security activity was documented mainly through government announcements; independent evaluations of capability and effectiveness remain limited.
- The president’s personal role is difficult to separate from the Foreign Ministry, CNSNS, CFE, National Guard, Defense Ministry, and professional diplomatic officials.

## 5. Important questions for deeper research

1. What did the IAEA conclude about Mexico’s 2023 safeguards implementation and whether it could provide broader assurance regarding declared nuclear material?
2. What corrective actions followed the October 2023 Ir-192 theft, and were there inspection, prosecution, or regulatory findings?
3. What were CNSNS’s 2023 budget, staffing, inspection, enforcement, and emergency-response figures?
4. Did López Obrador personally issue instructions affecting nuclear security, export controls, or strategic-risk policy?
5. Did Mexico submit its 2023 Biological Weapons Convention confidence-building declaration, and what facilities or activities did it disclose?
6. What national cybersecurity policy or legal framework existed in 2023, and how independent were military cyber units from civilian oversight?
7. Did Mexico’s presidency of the TPNW meeting produce negotiated commitments, reporting requirements, or implementation measures beyond diplomatic language?
8. What evidence exists regarding Mexico’s participation in nuclear-export-control regimes and enforcement against illicit procurement networks?
9. Did the government’s expanding military role create new cyber, radiological, or dual-use governance risks through reduced civilian oversight?
10. What was Mexico’s end-of-period catastrophic-risk posture compared with the inherited 2018 baseline?

## 6. Corroborating sources

Opened or examined mainly for corroboration:

- [UNODA, TPNW Second Meeting of States Parties](https://meetings.unoda.org/tpnw/tpnw-msp-2023)
- [UNODA 2023 Yearbook](https://yearbook.unoda.org/en-us/2023/chapter1/)
- [UNODA, Treaty of Tlatelolco](https://www.unoda.org/en/nwfz/treaty-tlatelolco)
- [SIPRI, World nuclear forces, January 2023](https://www.sipri.org/yearbook/2023/07)
- [UNODA, Biological Weapons Convention confidence-building measures](https://disarmament.unoda.org/en/our-work/weapons-mass-destruction/biological-weapons/confidence-building-measures)
- [IAEA, Mexico country nuclear-power profile](https://cnpp.iaea.org/public/countries/MX/profile/preview)
- [IAEA, Mexico legal and treaty status](https://ola.iaea.org/Applications/FactSheets/Country/Detail?code=MX)

## 7. Promising leads still needing inspection

- Mexico’s 2023 IAEA safeguards conclusions and any Board of Governors documentation.
- CNSNS annual report, inspection logs, enforcement actions, and radioactive-source security records for 2023.
- The full Mexican report to the Biological Weapons Convention confidence-building-measures process.
- Mexico’s 2023 national report under UN Security Council Resolution 1540.
- Mexico’s 2023 nuclear-export-control legislation and customs enforcement data.
- Full outcome document of the Second Meeting of States Parties to the TPNW.
- Mexican federal cybersecurity strategy, budgets, incident reports, and civilian oversight arrangements.
- Any 2023 IAEA or WANO peer-review material concerning Laguna Verde.
- Records on the appointment, independence, and technical qualifications of CNSNS leadership during the López Obrador administration.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 2B ---

# Chapter 2B — International Peace, Restraint, and Lawful Security

Period: 2023–2023  
Ruler: Andrés Manuel López Obrador, President of Mexico  
Prompt version: `chapter_research_prompt_v2`  
Lens presentation: `layered_lenses_v1`

## 1. Orientation

Mexico was not a belligerent in an interstate war during 2023 and did not conduct a documented cross-border military campaign. The principal international-security exposures were:

- Russia’s invasion of Ukraine and Mexico’s proposed diplomatic mediation;
- Mexico’s strained relationship with Peru after Pedro Castillo’s removal;
- security and migration cooperation with the United States;
- arms trafficking, fentanyl precursors, and transnational criminal networks;
- Mexico’s expanding domestic military role and associated accountability concerns.

López Obrador controlled Mexico’s executive foreign-policy agenda and publicly shaped its international-security rhetoric. His government generally favored non-intervention, neutrality, dialogue, and non-military solutions. It did not provide weapons to Ukraine and supported a ceasefire and mediation. However, his repeated attacks on Peru’s government were viewed by Peru as interference in its internal affairs, and his defense of a Russian military contingent at Mexico’s 2023 Independence parade complicated Mexico’s claim to principled neutrality.

The conflict-exposure gate is therefore limited: Mexico was not a direct party to an interstate war, but the president made consequential choices concerning a major international war, regional diplomatic disputes, arms trafficking, migration, and security cooperation.

## 2. Disposition by lens

| Lens | Disposition | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| 2B.1 Peaceful alternatives | Evidence developed | WEB-2B-001, WEB-2B-002 |
| 2B.2 Starting or prolonging war | No evidence Mexico initiated or prolonged an interstate war | WEB-2B-001, WEB-2B-004 |
| 2B.3 Honest justification for force | No Mexican use of interstate force identified; rhetoric was mixed | WEB-2B-001, WEB-2B-003 |
| 2B.4 Civilian and prisoner protection | Limited international evidence; migration and regional security records more relevant | WEB-2B-005 |
| 2B.5 Control of proxies and arms | Evidence of cooperation against arms trafficking; no evidence of proxy warfare | WEB-2B-005 |
| 2B.6 Scrutiny of war claims | Evidence mixed: official diplomacy was public, but presidential rhetoric was partisan and difficult to verify | WEB-2B-001, WEB-2B-003 |
| 2B.7 Ceasefires and settlements | Mexico proposed dialogue and a ceasefire for Ukraine, but no settlement resulted | WEB-2B-001, WEB-2B-002 |
| 2B.8 Responsible military resources | Military expenditure and military institutional expansion increased; international defensive necessity was not demonstrated | WEB-2B-004, WEB-2B-006 |
| 2B.9 Accountability and remedy | International accountability evidence is limited; military accountability concerns are relevant context | WEB-2B-006 |
| 2B.10 Lasting international peace | No clear measurable improvement in international peace attributable to López Obrador; diplomatic restraint coexisted with regional friction | WEB-2B-001 through WEB-2B-006 |

## 3. Evidence records

### WEB-2B-001 — Mexico proposed a peaceful diplomatic path for the Ukraine war

**Claim and significance:** In July 2023, Mexico reiterated support for a ceasefire between Russia and Ukraine and offered to help organize talks involving both parties. The government referred to López Obrador’s earlier proposal for a group of countries and international actors to mediate.

**Strongest source:** *México a favor de que se establezca un cese al fuego entre Rusia y Ucrania y en disposición de colaborar en pláticas de paz que involucren a ambas naciones*, Mexican Foreign Ministry, July 31, 2023. [Direct source](https://www.gob.mx/sre/prensa/mexico-a-favor-de-establezca-un-cese-al-fuego-entre-rusia-y-ucrania-y-en-disposicion-de-colaborar-en-platicas-de-paz-que-involucren-a-ambas-naciones?idiom=es)

**Locator:** Foreign Ministry communiqué No.  — July 31, 2023; paragraphs describing support for a ceasefire and willingness to participate in talks involving Russia and Ukraine.

**Period status:** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution:** Strong at the policy level. The proposal was publicly identified with López Obrador and implemented through the Foreign Ministry.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** Ukraine did not accept Mexico as a mediator on the terms proposed, and no ceasefire or settlement resulted. Ukraine’s president stated in May 2023 that mediation proposals must respect Ukraine’s sovereignty and territorial integrity and objected to mediation with the aggressor on terms that bypassed Ukraine’s own peace formula. [Ukraine presidential office](https://www.president.gov.ua/en/news/u-poserednictvi-z-agresorom-nemaye-neobhidnosti-vodnochas-uk-82869)

**Source cautions:** The Mexican communiqué is an official statement of intent, not evidence that the proposal had realistic negotiating leverage or achieved an operational result.

**Independent support:** Mexico’s September 2023 UN Security Council statement described Mexico’s commitment to dialogue, multilateralism, and opposition to explosive weapons in populated areas. [Mexican Foreign Ministry](https://www.gob.mx/sre/documentos/remarks-by-the-secretary-of-foreign-affairs-alicia-barcena-ibarra)

**Relevant lenses:** 2B.1, 2B.3, 2B.6, 2B.7, 2B.10.

---

### WEB-2B-002 — Mexico’s Ukraine position emphasized dialogue and non-military restraint

**Claim and significance:** Mexico’s official position in 2023 rejected supplying weapons and emphasized neutrality, dialogue, and a negotiated solution to the Ukraine war. In September, Foreign Secretary Alicia Bárcena stated at the UN that Mexico condemned explosive weapons in populated areas, cluster munitions, and anti-personnel mines, and supported effective multilateralism.

**Strongest source:** *Remarks by the Secretary of Foreign Affairs Alicia Bárcena Ibarra*, Mexican Foreign Ministry, September 20, 2023. [Direct source](https://www.gob.mx/sre/documentos/remarks-by-the-secretary-of-foreign-affairs-alicia-barcena-ibarra)

**Locator:** Remarks delivered at the UN Security Council meeting on Ukraine during the 78th UN General Assembly session; sections concerning multilateralism, explosive weapons, cluster munitions, mines, and Mexico’s peace initiative.

**Period status:** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution:** Moderate to strong. The Foreign Ministry represented the government’s position during López Obrador’s presidency, although the statement was delivered by Bárcena rather than the president.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** Mexico’s neutrality was criticized because it did not join Western sanctions against Russia and because López Obrador criticized U.S. military aid to Ukraine. His government also invited a Russian military contingent to the September 2023 Independence parade. Associated Press reported that Mexico had condemned the invasion while maintaining neutrality and refusing to participate in sanctions. [AP](https://apnews.com/article/6eb8345ba6827282ec40bf33891d6f22e)

**Source cautions:** Official diplomatic rhetoric demonstrates stated policy but cannot by itself establish equal treatment of aggressor and victim or the practical effect of neutrality.

**Independent support:** Ukrainian presidential office statement on peace proposals and territorial sovereignty; AP reporting on the Russian contingent.

**Relevant lenses:** 2B.1, 2B.2, 2B.3, 2B.6, 2B.7, 2B.10.

---

### WEB-2B-003 — Russian military contingent at Mexico’s Independence parade created a neutrality controversy

**Claim and significance:** López Obrador defended the presence of Russian soldiers at Mexico’s September 2023 Independence Day military parade. He argued that all countries with which Mexico had diplomatic relations had been invited. Ukraine’s ambassador criticized the Russian contingent because Russia was waging war against Ukraine.

**Strongest source:** *Mexican president defends inclusion of Russian military contingent in Independence parade*, Associated Press, September 18, 2023. [Direct source](https://apnews.com/article/6eb8345ba6827282ec40bf33891d6f22e)

**Locator:** AP report, paragraphs describing López Obrador’s defense of the invitation, Mexico’s neutrality, Ukraine’s ambassador’s criticism, and Mexico’s refusal to join sanctions.

**Period status:** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution:** Strong. The president personally defended the decision and framed it as a diplomatic-invitation policy.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** Mexico had also condemned Russia’s invasion and did not send military forces or weapons to Russia. The government could plausibly characterize the parade as an inclusive diplomatic event rather than an endorsement of Russian conduct.

**Source cautions:** AP is independent reporting but relies on public statements; it does not establish the parade’s diplomatic effect beyond the documented controversy.

**Independent support:** Mexican Foreign Ministry statements condemning attacks on civilians and supporting UN principles; Ukrainian ambassadorial statement reported by AP.

**Relevant lenses:** 2B.3, 2B.6, 2B.10.

---

### WEB-2B-004 — López Obrador’s conflict with Peru damaged regional diplomatic relations

**Claim and significance:** In 2023, López Obrador repeatedly denounced Peru’s government after the removal and imprisonment of President Pedro Castillo. Peru withdrew its ambassador, leaving relations formally at the level of chargé d’affaires. López Obrador called President Dina Boluarte illegitimate and criticized the Peruvian Congress and security response.

**Strongest source:** *Mexico regrets Peru’s decision to withdraw ambassador*, Associated Press, February 25, 2023. [Direct source](https://apnews.com/article/9aca91c08fd75905c52f32e801aacdde)

**Locator:** AP report, paragraphs 1744–1778, documenting the ambassadorial withdrawal, López Obrador’s statements, Peru’s accusation of interference, and the protest death toll.

**Period status:** Target-year evidence concerning an inherited crisis that began in December 2022.

**Ruler attribution:** Strong. The diplomatic rupture followed the president’s personal public statements and his government’s policy toward Castillo and Boluarte.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** Mexico argued that it was defending democratic principles and criticizing lethal repression, not seeking territorial or military coercion. The Foreign Ministry said it would keep communication channels open and hoped for a democratic agreement.

**Source cautions:** AP reports competing political claims and does not adjudicate the constitutional legality of Castillo’s removal or Peru’s subsequent government.

**Independent support:** *El País*, reporting on López Obrador’s cancellation of participation in APEC and escalation of the dispute. [El País](https://elpais.com/mexico/2023-09-21/lopez-obrador-escala-su-conflicto-diplomatico-con-dina-boluarte-y-cancela-su-participacion-en-la-apec.html)

**Relevant lenses:** 2B.1, 2B.3, 2B.6, 2B.10.

---

### WEB-2B-005 — Mexico and the United States expanded cooperative security arrangements

**Claim and significance:** In March 2023, Mexico and the United States launched the second phase of the Bicentennial Framework for Security. The agreement addressed fentanyl production, trafficking of high-caliber weapons and ammunition, transnational organized crime, and shared security responsibilities. In December, the two governments also reaffirmed cooperation on migration, human smuggling, trafficking, and legal pathways.

**Strongest source:** *Joint Statement México–United States: Launch of Phase II of the Bicentennial Framework for Security*, Government of Mexico, March 9, 2023. [Direct source](https://www.gob.mx/amlo/prensa/joint-statement-mexico-united-states)

**Locator:** Statement describing the March 9, 2023 meeting and the agreed priorities of fentanyl, arms trafficking, ammunition, and organized crime.

**Period status:** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution:** Strong at the executive-policy level. López Obrador hosted the meeting and his administration negotiated the framework.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** Security cooperation with the United States can reduce cross-border crime and arms flows, but it can also increase enforcement pressure, militarization, and migrant-control burdens. The public statement does not provide independent outcome measures.

**Source cautions:** The source is a joint official statement and is therefore strong for commitments but weak for implementation, casualty, and human-rights outcomes.

**Independent support:** U.S.–Mexico High-Level Security Dialogue 2023 materials; December 2023 Mexico–U.S. migration statement. [SRE](https://www.gob.mx/sre/prensa/mexico-and-united-states-reaffirm-their-shared-commitment-to-orderly-humanitarian-and-regular-migration?idiom=en)

**Relevant lenses:** 2B.1, 2B.4, 2B.5, 2B.7, 2B.8, 2B.10.

---

### WEB-2B-006 — Military spending increased in a context of expanding military roles

**Claim and significance:** SIPRI estimated Mexico’s military expenditure at approximately $11.8 billion in 2023, a 1.5 percent decline from 2022 but a 55 percent increase over 2014. This spending occurred while López Obrador’s government continued transferring civilian responsibilities to the armed forces and expanding military administration of security and infrastructure.

**Strongest source:** *Global military spending surges amid war, rising tensions and insecurity*, SIPRI, April 22, 2024. [Direct source](https://www.sipri.org/media/press-release/2024/global-military-spending-surges-amid-war-rising-tensions-and-insecurity)

**Locator:** Mexico entry stating approximately $11.8 billion in 2023, a 1.5 percent year-on-year decline, and a 55 percent increase from 2014.

**Period status:** Target-year outcome/resource evidence.

**Ruler attribution:** Moderate. SIPRI measures national military expenditure, while López Obrador’s government made the political decision to expand military responsibilities. Attribution of each budget item to international-security needs is not established.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** Mexico’s military spending was not among the world’s largest as a share of GDP, and the 2023 figure declined from 2022. The government argued that military involvement addressed organized crime and national infrastructure needs.

**Source cautions:** SIPRI’s figures use comparable methodology but may include or exclude security-related expenditure differently across countries; the data do not show whether spending was lawful, proportionate, or effective.

**Independent support:** SIPRI’s Mexico 2023 country expenditure page; IACHR findings that Mexico continued strengthening SEDENA and military public-security functions. [SIPRI](https://www.sipri.org/node/6992), [IACHR](https://www.oas.org/en/iachr/docs/annual/2023/chapters/IA2023_Cap_5_Mexico_ENG.PDF)

**Relevant lenses:** 2B.4, 2B.5, 2B.8, 2B.9, 2B.10.

---

## 4. Information environment and evidence limitations

The international record was comparatively accessible because foreign-policy statements, UN debates, bilateral agreements, and diplomatic incidents were publicly documented. The main limitations were:

- official Mexican statements emphasized neutrality and peace but did not establish practical influence over belligerents;
- the Ukraine mediation proposal was documented as an initiative, but evidence of serious acceptance by Russia or Ukraine was absent;
- Mexico’s nonparticipation in sanctions and criticism of Western arms support made its neutrality politically contested;
- the Peru dispute involved competing constitutional and democratic narratives that require separate legal analysis;
- military-spending data do not distinguish international defense from domestic policing, infrastructure, or institutional expansion;
- public bilateral-security statements provide commitments but limited evidence about civilian harm, migrant treatment, arms-diversion prevention, or operational results;
- the strongest evidence of international restraint concerns what Mexico did not do—no interstate war, no documented military intervention, and no weapons shipment to Ukraine—which should not be converted automatically into proof of positive peacebuilding.

## 5. Important questions for deeper research

1. Did Mexico vote consistently in 2023 for UN resolutions condemning Russia’s invasion and protecting Ukraine’s territorial integrity?
2. What precisely was contained in López Obrador’s proposed Ukraine peace plan, and was it compatible with Ukraine’s sovereignty and the UN Charter?
3. Did Russia, Ukraine, or other governments formally respond to Mexico’s July ceasefire and mediation proposal?
4. Did Mexico’s refusal to join sanctions materially assist Russia, or was its economic effect negligible?
5. What legal basis did López Obrador invoke for his statements and policy toward Peru, and did Mexico violate any regional non-intervention commitments?
6. What measurable outcomes followed the second phase of the Bicentennial Framework?
7. How did Mexico’s cooperation with the United States affect migrants, asylum seekers, and third-country nationals in 2023?
8. Which military expenditures in 2023 were directed toward external defense, domestic security, infrastructure, or commercial enterprises?
9. Did military expansion reduce or increase the risk of unlawful force and weaken civilian accountability?
10. Did Mexico leave its regional diplomatic relationships more stable at the end of 2023 than at the beginning?

## 7. Promising leads still needing inspection

- Mexico’s complete 2023 UN voting record on Ukraine and other armed conflicts.
- Full text and diplomatic circulation history of López Obrador’s Ukraine peace proposal.
- Government records on Mexican arms exports, arms imports, and military assistance to foreign states.
- Mexican and Peruvian legal submissions concerning non-intervention, diplomatic representation, and asylum.
- 2023 implementation reports for the Bicentennial Framework, including seizures, prosecutions, human-rights safeguards, and cross-border intelligence.
- Detailed Mexican defense budget execution data separating military, National Guard, infrastructure, and civilian-administration functions.
- UNHCR, IOM, and Mexican refugee-commission evidence on the humanitarian effects of Mexico–U.S. migration-security cooperation.
- Any 2023 regional peacekeeping, mediation, or confidence-building initiatives involving Mexico.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 3B ---

# Chapter 3B — Domestic Safety, Restraint, and Protection

Period: 2023–2023  
Ruler: Andrés Manuel López Obrador, President of Mexico  
Prompt version: `chapter_research_prompt_v2`  
Lens presentation: `layered_lenses_v1`

## 1. Orientation

Mexico entered 2023 with an inherited crisis of organized-crime violence, disappearances, military involvement in public security, weak investigations, and high impunity. López Obrador had strong authority over federal security institutions, the National Guard, the Army, Navy, National Search Commission, federal prosecutors, and federal protection mechanisms. His practical authority was limited by federalism, state and municipal police, autonomous courts and human-rights bodies, organized crime, and the military’s institutional power.

The administration continued relying heavily on the armed forces for domestic security. The Supreme Court struck down the formal transfer of the National Guard to the Defense Ministry in April 2023, but military personnel remained deeply involved in public security. Evidence from the UN, IACHR, Amnesty International, Human Rights Watch, AP, and Article 19 documents persistent killings, disappearances, arbitrary detention, torture allegations, attacks on journalists, violence against women, and weak remedy.

There were also positive or mitigating developments: homicide rates declined modestly from 2022; federal authorities created and maintained search and protection mechanisms; the Army publicly referred some incidents to civilian prosecutors; and courts sometimes constrained executive and military arrangements. However, the record does not establish a durable reduction in exposure to state and non-state violence during 2023.

## 2. Disposition by lens

| Lens | Disposition | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| 3B.1 Protection from state abuse | Evidence of persistent abuse and inadequate remedy | WEB-3B-001, WEB-3B-002, WEB-3B-003 |
| 3B.2 Control of coercive forces | Adverse evidence; military control remained incomplete | WEB-3B-002, WEB-3B-004 |
| 3B.3 Incitement and targeted hatred | Evidence of stigmatizing presidential rhetoric; no direct incitement finding | WEB-3B-005 |
| 3B.4 Complaints and independent oversight | Oversight existed but was obstructed or ineffective in important areas | WEB-3B-001, WEB-3B-002, WEB-3B-003 |
| 3B.5 Emergency and surveillance powers | Evidence of arbitrary detention, surveillance, and legal overreach | WEB-3B-002, WEB-3B-006 |
| 3B.6 Safety without state terror | Persistent criminal violence alongside militarized security | WEB-3B-006, WEB-3B-007 |
| 3B.7 Protection of vulnerable groups | Serious gender-based violence and weak protection | WEB-3B-001, WEB-3B-005 |
| 3B.8 Safe protest and dissent | Evidence of excessive force, criminalization, and official stigmatization | WEB-3B-005 |
| 3B.9 Restrained crisis response | Mixed: some referrals and mechanisms, but poor transparency and remedy | WEB-3B-003, WEB-3B-005 |
| 3B.10 Lasting domestic safety | No demonstrated durable improvement; evidence remains adverse and mixed | WEB-3B-001 through WEB-3B-007 |

## 3. Evidence records

### WEB-3B-001 — Disappearance crisis and near-total impunity

**Claim and significance:** On October 3, 2023, the UN Committee on Enforced Disappearances reported that Mexico’s national registry contained 111,540 disappeared or missing persons as of September 12, 2023. The Committee criticized unreliable updating methods, inadequate investigations, low conviction numbers, and near-total impunity.

**Strongest source:** *El Comité de la ONU contra la Desaparición Forzada publica observaciones sobre Mauritania, Nigeria, México y Países Bajos*, OHCHR Mexico, October 3, 2023. [Direct source](https://hchr.org.mx/comunicados/el-comite-de-la-onu-contra-la-desaparicion-forzada-publica-observaciones-sobre-mauritania-nigeria-mexico-y-paises-bajos/)

**Locator:** Paras. 74–81.

**Period status:** Target-year evidence, concerning a crisis accumulated over many years.

**Ruler attribution:** Moderate to strong. López Obrador’s administration controlled the National Search Commission, federal investigative agencies, registry policy, and national response, though many disappearances were committed by criminal groups or state and local actors.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** The government maintained a national registry, search commission, search programs, and identification initiatives. The Committee acknowledged positive measures but found them insufficient.

**Source cautions:** The Committee’s figures include all registered missing persons, not only enforced disappearances attributable to the state. The registry’s methodology was itself under criticism.

**Independent support:** Human Rights Watch, *World Report 2024: Mexico*; IACHR, *Annual Report 2023, Chapter V: Mexico*.

**Relevant lenses:** 3B.1, 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.6, 3B.7, 3B.9, 3B.10.

---

### WEB-3B-002 — Militarized security and weak accountability

**Claim and significance:** During 2023, Mexico continued deploying the armed forces and National Guard in public security. Human Rights Watch reported more than 266,000 soldiers, marines, and National Guard members deployed domestically as of August 2023. It also reported that the Army had killed 5,488 civilians from 2007 through July 2023 and that such killings were rarely independently investigated.

**Strongest source:** *World Report 2024: Mexico*, Human Rights Watch, 2024. [Direct source](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/mexico)

**Locator:** Paras. 70–83, especially paras. 79–82.

**Period status:** 2023 outcome and institutional evidence, with longer-term baseline data.

**Ruler attribution:** Strong institutionally. López Obrador made military-led public security a defining policy and expanded the armed forces’ domestic role.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** The government argued that military deployment was necessary against heavily armed criminal groups. The Supreme Court struck down the formal transfer of the National Guard to SEDENA in April 2023, demonstrating an institutional check.

**Source cautions:** HRW is an advocacy organization, although it cites government data and court decisions. The Army’s cumulative civilian-killing figure covers multiple administrations and does not isolate 2023.

**Independent support:** Amnesty International’s 2023 Mexico submission; IACHR Annual Report 2023; AP reporting on the Nuevo Laredo shooting.

**Relevant lenses:** 3B.1, 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.5, 3B.6, 3B.9, 3B.10.

---

### WEB-3B-003 — Nuevo Laredo soldiers killed five civilians

**Claim and significance:** In February 2023, Mexico’s Defense Ministry acknowledged that soldiers opened fire on a pickup truck in Nuevo Laredo, killing five men and wounding one. The Army said troops believed the vehicle was behaving evasively after a crash; residents and a local rights group said the victims were unarmed and that the killings were arbitrary. The Defense Ministry said it cooperated with civilian prosecutors.

**Strongest source:** *Mexican army confirms troops fired on civilians, killing 5*, Associated Press, February 28, 2023. [Direct source](https://apnews.com/article/mexico-border-violence-army-4929d99c4361133b2638562028afbfcc)

**Locator:** AP report paragraphs 1667–1693.

**Period status:** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution:** Moderate to strong. The incident involved federal soldiers operating under the administration’s militarized security policy; personal presidential direction was not established.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** The Army claimed it heard gunshots and that the vehicle failed to stop. The case was reportedly referred to civilian prosecutors and military prosecutors.

**Source cautions:** The facts surrounding the shooting were disputed and the cited report does not establish the final legal outcome.

**Independent support:** Human Rights Watch reporting on Nuevo Laredo and prior military disappearances; Mexican Defense Ministry statements.

**Relevant lenses:** 3B.1, 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.6, 3B.9.

---

### WEB-3B-004 — Ayotzinapa investigation obstructed by military institutions

**Claim and significance:** López Obrador created a truth commission and renewed the investigation into the 2014 disappearance of 43 Ayotzinapa students. In 2023, independent experts said the investigation could not continue because the military withheld evidence. Human Rights Watch reported that the president did not use his authority to force the military to provide the missing documents.

**Strongest source:** *Ayotzinapa, Mexico’s Army, and López Obrador’s Silence*, Human Rights Watch, September 26, 2023. [Direct source](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/09/26/ayotzinapa-mexicos-army-and-lopez-obradors-silence)

**Locator:** Paras. 650–659.

**Period status:** Target-year evidence about an inherited atrocity and continuing investigation.

**Ruler attribution:** Strong. López Obrador established the investigative framework and had executive authority over federal institutions, although the original disappearance preceded his presidency.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** His government made important initial commitments, created the truth commission, and pursued arrests and official findings. The obstruction was attributed particularly to military institutions and did not necessarily originate with the president personally.

**Source cautions:** HRW is an advocacy organization and relies partly on expert and investigative reporting; the precise legal status of every withheld document remains unresolved.

**Independent support:** GIEI reports; AP reporting; IACHR materials.

**Relevant lenses:** 3B.1, 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.9, 3B.10.

---

### WEB-3B-005 — Violence against women and repression of protest

**Claim and significance:** Amnesty International reported that between 2018 and May 2023 at least 20,292 women were killed in Mexico, with 5,065 cases investigated as alleged femicides. It also documented stigmatization, excessive force, arbitrary arrests, sexual violence, and criminalization affecting women protesters and human-rights defenders. Amnesty identified presidential morning conferences as a source of stigmatizing rhetoric toward critics, journalists, NGOs, and protesters.

**Strongest source:** *Mexico: Rushing Headfirst into an Abyss of Human Rights?*, Amnesty International, July 2023. [Direct source](https://www.amnesty.org/en/wp-content/uploads/2023/07/AMR4169922023ENGLISH.pdf)

**Locator:** PDF pp. 2–4, paras. 14–20; especially paras. 17–19.

**Period status:** Target-year publication using cumulative 2018–May 2023 data; some protest examples predate 2023.

**Ruler attribution:** Moderate. Gender violence was largely decentralized and involved state and local authorities, but the federal government controlled national policy, funding, public rhetoric, and federal protection mechanisms.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** Mexico had gender-violence alerts, legal protections, and federal mechanisms. The evidence does not establish that López Obrador directly ordered violence against women protesters or personally incited attacks.

**Source cautions:** Amnesty’s submission is an advocacy document prepared for a UN review; some examples are retrospective and not limited to 2023.

**Independent support:** U.S. State Department, *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Mexico*; IACHR; Mexican public-security statistics.

**Relevant lenses:** 3B.3, 3B.6, 3B.7, 3B.8, 3B.9.

---

### WEB-3B-006 — Arbitrary detention and noncompliance with human-rights rulings

**Claim and significance:** Human Rights Watch reported that arbitrary detention remained widespread and could lead to ill-treatment, torture, enforced disappearance, and arbitrary executions. It also reported that Mexico continued using mandatory pretrial detention and arraigo despite Inter-American Court rulings that these practices violated human-rights standards. Military and naval forces reportedly detained civilians without consistently recording those detentions in the National Detention Registry.

**Strongest source:** *World Report 2024: Mexico*, Human Rights Watch, 2024. [Direct source](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/mexico)

**Locator:** Paras. 70–76.

**Period status:** 2023 evidence concerning continuing legal and institutional practices.

**Ruler attribution:** Moderate to strong. López Obrador’s administration supported or retained the legal and security framework, although constitutional reform and judicial compliance involved Congress and courts.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** In July 2023, a federal appeals court reportedly allowed judges to hear appeals and order conditional release in some mandatory-pretrial-detention cases. This shows some judicial correction.

**Source cautions:** HRW summarizes complex constitutional and Inter-American legal developments; individual detention practices require case-level records.

**Independent support:** Inter-American Court rulings; Amnesty International; U.S. State Department.

**Relevant lenses:** 3B.1, 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.5, 3B.9.

---

### WEB-3B-007 — Criminal violence remained severe despite modest homicide decline

**Claim and significance:** INEGI recorded 15,082 homicides in Mexico from January through June 2023, a preliminary rate of 12 per 100,000 inhabitants. Firearms were used in 71.3 percent of cases. The rate was lower than in the comparable 2022 period but remained extremely high.

**Strongest source:** *Deaths due to homicide from January to June 2023 (preliminary)*, INEGI, January 23, 2024. [Direct source](https://en.www.inegi.org.mx/app/saladeprensa/noticia/8768)

**Locator:** INEGI press release summary and preliminary first-semester tables.

**Period status:** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution:** Moderate. Homicides were largely produced by organized crime and state/local conditions, but federal security policy and military deployment were central to the president’s program.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** The homicide rate declined from 2022. The data do not establish that federal policy caused either the decline or the remaining violence.

**Source cautions:** The figures were preliminary registered-death statistics and do not identify perpetrators or policy causes.

**Independent support:** U.S. State Department; IACHR; Human Rights Watch.

**Relevant lenses:** 3B.2, 3B.6, 3B.7, 3B.10.

---

### WEB-3B-008 — Attacks on journalists and weak protection

**Claim and significance:** Article 19 recorded 272 attacks against journalists and media outlets during the first half of 2023, approximately one attack every 16 hours. It attributed 140 attacks to public authorities, including federal, state, and municipal officials. The report documented intimidation, harassment, threats, and possible freedom-of-expression-related killings and disappearances.

**Strongest source:** *Informe semestral 2023: violencia contra la prensa entre ataques, estigmatización y ausencia del Estado*, Article 19 Mexico and Central America, September 19, 2023. [Direct source](https://articulo19.org/informe-semestral-2023-violencia-contra-la-prensa-entre-ataques-estigmatizacion-y-ausencia-del-estado/)

**Locator:** Paras. 61–72.

**Period status:** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution:** Moderate. Article 19 attributed most attacks to state authorities, but state-level officials accounted for the largest share. López Obrador’s federal rhetoric and protection mechanisms are relevant, but local perpetrators and organized crime complicate attribution.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** The federal Protection Mechanism existed and received budget and staffing increases. Article 19’s figures include incidents with varying severity and do not prove that every attack was ordered or tolerated by the president.

**Source cautions:** Article 19 is an independent human-rights organization; its incident-counting methodology should be compared with CPJ, RSF, and government figures.

**Independent support:** Human Rights Watch; Amnesty International; IACHR; AP.

**Relevant lenses:** 3B.3, 3B.4, 3B.7, 3B.8, 3B.10.

## 4. Information environment and evidence limitations

The evidence environment was unusually difficult:

- official homicide, disappearance, detention, and femicide data were fragmented across federal, state, and municipal institutions;
- the UN CED specifically questioned the reliability and transparency of Mexico’s missing-person registry;
- military institutions controlled information about operations and repeatedly resisted disclosure;
- victims’ families and journalists faced intimidation, making underreporting likely;
- presidential rhetoric and independent reporting were highly polarized;
- many incidents were decentralized, so national attribution must distinguish federal command from state, municipal, criminal, and military conduct;
- official institutions sometimes documented prosecutions or referrals, but final case outcomes were difficult to obtain;
- later retrospective reports are useful but should not be treated as contemporaneous 2023 evidence.

## 5. Important questions for deeper research

1. What were the final 2023 homicide, femicide, disappearance, torture, and unlawful-killing figures?
2. How many 2023 deaths involving federal security forces were independently investigated, prosecuted, and punished?
3. Did the Nuevo Laredo case result in civilian charges, convictions, compensation, or disciplinary action?
4. What documents did the Army withhold in the Ayotzinapa case, and what remedies were available to victims?
5. Did the National Search Commission’s 2023 registry review remove or reclassify cases, and was the methodology independently audited?
6. What was the federal government’s direct contribution to state and municipal policing resources?
7. How many journalists, defenders, women protesters, migrants, and Indigenous people received protection, and how many were harmed despite it?
8. Did López Obrador’s public attacks on critics produce identifiable security consequences, such as threats or attacks by officials, police, or aligned groups?
9. What were the implementation and enforcement records for the National Detention Registry?
10. Did the Supreme Court’s April 2023 ruling materially alter National Guard command or only its formal legal structure?
11. What was the geographic distribution of violence and whether federal military deployment changed outcomes?
12. Did domestic safety at the end of 2023 measurably differ from the 2018 inherited baseline after adjusting for reporting and registry changes?

## 6. Corroborating sources

Opened or examined mainly for corroboration:

- [U.S. State Department, 2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Mexico](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107784.html)
- [IACHR Annual Report 2023, Mexico](https://www.oas.org/en/iachr/docs/annual/2023/chapters/IA2023_Cap_5_Mexico_ENG.PDF)
- [Human Rights Watch, World Report 2024: Mexico](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/mexico)
- [Human Rights Watch, Ayotzinapa, Mexico’s Army, and López Obrador’s Silence](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/09/26/ayotzinapa-mexicos-army-and-lopez-obradors-silence)
- [Amnesty International, Mexico: Rushing Headfirst into an Abyss of Human Rights?](https://www.amnesty.org/en/wp-content/uploads/2023/07/AMR4169922023ENGLISH.pdf)
- [Article 19, 2023 semiannual press-violence report](https://articulo19.org/informe-semestral-2023-violencia-contra-la-prensa-entre-ataques-estigmatizacion-y-ausencia-del-estado/)
- [INEGI, preliminary January–June 2023 homicide statistics](https://en.www.inegi.org.mx/app/saladeprensa/noticia/8768)
- [AP, Nuevo Laredo military shooting](https://apnews.com/article/mexico-border-violence-army-4929d99c4361133b2638562028afbfcc)

## 7. Promising leads still needing inspection

- Full 2023 National Search Commission registry, methodology, and audit records.
- Federal Prosecutor’s Office records on disappearances and military-related killings.
- Final legal outcome of the Nuevo Laredo shooting.
- Full 2023 National Human Rights Commission complaint and recommendation database.
- National Detention Registry completeness audits for Army, Navy, and National Guard detentions.
- Full Article 19, CPJ, RSF, and Protection Mechanism datasets for 2023.
- State-level femicide, protest-policing, and violence-against-women records.
- Supreme Court judgment invalidating the National Guard transfer to SEDENA.
- Records of federal funding, staffing, and performance indicators for the National Search Commission and journalist-protection mechanism.
- Independent forensic and investigative assessments of military operations in Nuevo Laredo and other high-violence areas.
