--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 4B ---

# Chapter 4B — Political Freedom versus Authoritarian Rule

Period: 2023–2023  
Ruler: Andrés Manuel López Obrador, President of Mexico  
Prompt version: `chapter_research_prompt_v2`  
Lens presentation: `layered_lenses_v1`

## 1. Orientation

López Obrador governed through a strong presidency, a Morena-led congressional coalition, highly centralized public communication, and extensive executive influence over federal agencies. Mexico remained a competitive electoral democracy: the president was constitutionally barred from immediate reelection, opposition parties operated openly, elections remained contested, and courts and autonomous institutions sometimes constrained the executive.

At the same time, 2023 showed substantial pressure on institutional checks. López Obrador backed “Plan B” electoral reforms that would have reduced the capacity and resources of the National Electoral Institute (INE); the Supreme Court later invalidated the package for serious legislative-procedure violations. The administration also left the transparency body INAI without a fully functioning quorum, attacked journalists and civil-society organizations, and was credibly linked through military institutions to surveillance of journalists and human-rights defenders.

The best-supported description is therefore competitive electoral democracy under significant executive pressure, institutional erosion, and hostile treatment of independent information. The 2023 evidence does not establish abolition of elections, a personal term-limit violation, or a completed transition to authoritarian rule.

## 2. Disposition by lens

| Lens | Disposition | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| 4B.1 Genuine electoral choice | Elections remained competitive, but proposed reforms threatened electoral oversight | WEB-4B-001, WEB-4B-002 |
| 4B.2 No entrenchment of power | Adverse evidence from Plan B and pressure on autonomous bodies; no reelection attempt | WEB-4B-001, WEB-4B-003 |
| 4B.3 Opposition and civic freedom | Opposition and civic groups remained active, but faced presidential hostility and harassment | WEB-4B-004, WEB-4B-005 |
| 4B.4 Independent checks | Courts constrained the executive; INAI and other institutions were weakened or obstructed | WEB-4B-002, WEB-4B-003 |
| 4B.5 Politically neutral institutions | Concerns about politicized appointments and military influence | WEB-4B-006 |
| 4B.6 Independent information and media | Strong evidence of verbal attacks, violence, surveillance, and dependence on state advertising | WEB-4B-004, WEB-4B-005 |
| 4B.7 Equal political rights | Formal rights remained, but journalists, activists, women protesters, and Indigenous/environmental groups faced unequal exposure | WEB-4B-005, WEB-4B-007 |
| 4B.8 Term limits and transfer | López Obrador did not seek reelection and publicly stated he would leave office in 2024 | WEB-4B-008 |
| 4B.9 Digital freedom and surveillance | Evidence of Pegasus surveillance by military institutions and inadequate oversight | WEB-4B-004, WEB-4B-005 |
| 4B.10 Lasting democratic resilience | Mixed: electoral and judicial checks survived, but executive pressure weakened institutional resilience | WEB-4B-001 through WEB-4B-008 |

## 3. Evidence records

### WEB-4B-001 — Plan B sought to weaken the electoral authority

**Claim and significance:** In February 2023, Mexico’s Senate approved López Obrador’s “Plan B” electoral reform by 72 votes to 50. The package would reduce the size and budget of the INE and limit its supervisory and sanctioning powers. The president backed the reform and argued that it would reduce costs and political privileges.

**Strongest source:** *Mexico’s Senate approves controversial electoral reform*, Associated Press, February 23, 2023. [Direct source](https://apnews.com/article/db6ed46583eb0601153e282ca29f5818)

**Locator:** AP report paragraphs 1781–1821.

**Period status:** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution:** Strong. The reform was proposed by López Obrador, backed by his party, and supported publicly by him.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** The administration argued that INE spending was excessive and that the reform improved efficiency. The reform did not permanently take effect because the Supreme Court invalidated it.

**Source cautions:** AP reports competing political claims; the constitutional merits are best established through the Supreme Court’s judgment.

**Independent support:** Supreme Court communiqué No. 222/2023, which invalidated the second part of Plan B for procedural violations. [SCJN](https://www.internet2.scjn.gob.mx/red2/comunicados/noticia.asp?id=7408)

**Relevant lenses:** 4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.4, 4B.8, 4B.10.

---

### WEB-4B-002 — Supreme Court invalidated Plan B and preserved an institutional check

**Claim and significance:** On June 22, 2023, the Supreme Court invalidated the second part of the electoral Plan B by a 9–2 vote. It found serious legislative-procedure violations, including inadequate notice, rushed consideration, failure to deliberate properly, and improper changes between congressional chambers.

**Strongest source:** *Invalida la Corte la segunda parte del paquete de reformas político-electorales 2022–2023 por violaciones al procedimiento legislativo*, Supreme Court of Justice of the Nation, June 22, 2023. [Direct source](https://www.internet2.scjn.gob.mx/red2/comunicados/noticia.asp?id=7408)

**Locator:** Communiqué No. 222/2023; listed procedural violations and the order restoring the pre-March 2, 2023 electoral rules.

**Period status:** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution:** Strong. The ruling concerned legislation politically sponsored by the president and his congressional coalition.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** The Court invalidated the reform primarily for procedural violations rather than resolving every policy objection to reducing INE costs and bureaucracy.

**Source cautions:** The communiqué is an official summary; the full judgment is the authoritative legal document.

**Independent support:** AP reporting on the Senate approval; INE legal challenges; electoral-law analysis.

**Relevant lenses:** 4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.4, 4B.10.

---

### WEB-4B-003 — INAI was left without full operating capacity

**Claim and significance:** In 2023, the Senate failed to fill vacancies on Mexico’s transparency and data-protection body, INAI. The Supreme Court recognized that the vacancies affected the institution’s proper integration and functioning and implicated its autonomy. López Obrador had rejected earlier nominees and repeatedly criticized INAI as unnecessary, costly, and opposed to his government.

**Strongest source:** *Controversia constitucional 280/2023*, Supreme Court of Justice of the Nation, 2023. [Direct source](https://www.internet2.scjn.gob.mx/red2/comunicados/comunicado.asp?id=7531)

**Locator:** Communiqué describing the three commissioner vacancies and their effect on INAI’s functioning and autonomy.

**Period status:** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution:** Moderate to strong. The vacancies resulted formally from Senate inaction, but the president publicly rejected nominees and opposed the institution.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** The Supreme Court allowed remaining commissioners to continue reviewing cases temporarily, and INAI remained constitutionally established.

**Source cautions:** The Supreme Court source records the legal institutional problem but does not independently establish that the president ordered the Senate’s inaction.

**Independent support:** *El País*, reporting that López Obrador rejected Senate nominees and had previously sought to eliminate autonomous bodies. [El País](https://elpais.com/mexico/2023-03-16/lopez-obrador-deja-al-organismo-de-transparencia-al-borde-de-la-inoperancia.html)

**Relevant lenses:** 4B.2, 4B.4, 4B.5, 4B.6, 4B.10.

---

### WEB-4B-004 — Presidential attacks on critical media and Article 19

**Claim and significance:** In 2023, López Obrador repeatedly attacked Article 19, Proceso, Animal Político, Aristegui Noticias, and journalist Nayeli Roldán after reporting on alleged military Pegasus surveillance. He called critical media biased and accused Article 19 of foreign interference. CPJ reported that these statements were followed by online abuse and threats against the targeted journalists and organizations.

**Strongest source:** *El presidente mexicano Andrés Manuel López Obrador critica a medios de prensa y a organización defensora de la libertad de expresión…*, Committee to Protect Journalists, May 19, 2023. [Direct source](https://cpj.org/es/2023/05/el-presidente-mexicano-andres-manuel-lopez-obrador-critica-a-medios-de-prensa-y-a-organizacion-defensora-de-la-libertad-de-expresion-por-la-cobertura-de-presuntos-operativos-de-espionaje-ilegales/)

**Locator:** Paras. 492–527.

**Period status:** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution:** Strong. The source documents the president’s personal statements and their immediate public consequences.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** López Obrador said he was responding to what he regarded as biased or inaccurate reporting and denied unlawful government surveillance. The media outlets remained active and continued publishing investigations.

**Source cautions:** CPJ is an advocacy organization; online threats cannot all be causally attributed to presidential statements, although the targeted statements and subsequent abuse are documented.

**Independent support:** Reuters Institute, *Digital News Report 2023: Mexico*; Freedom House; Freedom House, *Freedom on the Net 2023*.

**Relevant lenses:** 4B.3, 4B.6, 4B.9.

---

### WEB-4B-005 — Media pluralism persisted but operated under pressure

**Claim and significance:** The Reuters Institute’s 2023 Mexico report found that media outlets faced daily attacks from López Obrador, who used morning press conferences to pursue his agenda and target journalists and outlets. It also reported that Article 19 alleged a government disinformation strategy and reduced practical access to public information. Mexico retained diverse print and online viewpoints, but public trust in news had declined and the country ranked 128th of 180 in the 2023 RSF press-freedom index.

**Strongest source:** *Mexico*, Reuters Institute for the Study of Journalism, Digital News Report 2023, June 14, 2023. [Direct source](https://reutersinstitute.politics.ox.ac.uk/digital-news-report/2023/mexico)

**Locator:** Paras. 18–37 and 75–84.

**Period status:** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution:** Strong for presidential rhetoric; moderate for wider media outcomes because ownership, organized crime, state governments, and commercial pressures also mattered.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** Mexico retained numerous independent and digital-native outlets, broad internet access, active legal challenges, and significant online political debate.

**Source cautions:** The Mexico country page is authored by an academic journalist researcher and summarizes other sources; some claims, such as the proportion of false official information, derive from Article 19 and require separate examination.

**Independent support:** Article 19; CPJ; Freedom House.

**Relevant lenses:** 4B.3, 4B.6, 4B.9, 4B.10.

---

### WEB-4B-006 — Concerns about judicial and institutional politicization

**Claim and significance:** Freedom House assessed Mexico’s Supreme Court as generally independent but reported concerns that appointments viewed as close to the government had diminished judicial autonomy under López Obrador. It also reported continuing concerns about politicization, military influence, and weak accountability for abuses.

**Strongest source:** *Mexico: Freedom in the World 2023 Country Report*, Freedom House, 2023. [Direct source](https://freedomhouse.org/country/mexico/freedom-world/2023)

**Locator:** Paras. 157–186, especially the sections on judicial independence, military accountability, disappearances, and the National Guard.

**Period status:** 2023 country assessment, with evidence extending across the president’s term.

**Ruler attribution:** Moderate. The president nominated Supreme Court justices and promoted military-led security, but the judiciary and Congress had independent roles.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** In 2023, the Supreme Court invalidated Plan B and struck down the formal transfer of the National Guard to SEDENA, demonstrating that judicial independence was not eliminated.

**Source cautions:** Freedom House uses expert scoring and narrative assessment; it is useful for synthesis but not a substitute for appointment records or judgments.

**Independent support:** Supreme Court Plan B ruling; IACHR; AP.

**Relevant lenses:** 4B.2, 4B.4, 4B.5, 4B.10.

---

### WEB-4B-007 — Civic organizations and activists remained active but vulnerable

**Claim and significance:** Freedom House reported that Mexican civil-society organizations remained active and could criticize government policy, but faced violent resistance, public hostility, surveillance concerns, and vulnerability in disputes involving infrastructure and Indigenous or environmental rights. The federal protection mechanism protected hundreds but was viewed by rights groups as slow and neglected.

**Strongest source:** *Mexico: Freedom in the World 2023 Country Report*, Freedom House, 2023. [Direct source](https://freedomhouse.org/country/mexico/freedom-world/2023)

**Locator:** Paras. 143–151.

**Period status:** 2023 assessment with inherited conditions.

**Ruler attribution:** Moderate. Violence was often local or criminal, but federal rhetoric, surveillance policy, protection systems, and administrative pressure were within the national government’s influence.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** NGOs freely criticized state policies, and the protection mechanism provided concrete safeguards such as safe houses, panic buttons, and bodyguards.

**Source cautions:** The report includes older examples and broad country-level judgments; it does not isolate 2023 incidents.

**Independent support:** Article 19; Amnesty International; IACHR.

**Relevant lenses:** 4B.3, 4B.6, 4B.7, 4B.9, 4B.10.

---

### WEB-4B-008 — López Obrador respected the formal presidential term limit

**Claim and significance:** López Obrador was constitutionally ineligible for immediate reelection and publicly stated in 2023 that he would leave politics after his term ended in September 2024. No evidence was found that he sought to amend the Constitution to remain president or directly obstruct succession.

**Strongest source:** *Mexico’s Senate approves controversial electoral reform*, Associated Press, February 23, 2023. [Direct source](https://apnews.com/article/db6ed46583eb0601153e282ca29f5818)

**Locator:** AP caption and paragraph 1761 stating that López Obrador said he would withdraw from politics after his term ended in September 2024; paragraphs 1813–1815 describing his ineligibility for reelection.

**Period status:** Target-year evidence concerning the constitutional succession framework.

**Ruler attribution:** Strong for his stated intention; formal term-limit compliance was constitutional and institutional.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** His party’s dominance and his influence over Morena’s succession process raised concerns about informal continuation of influence, but that is distinct from personally retaining the presidency.

**Source cautions:** A public promise does not guarantee future compliance; the source does not establish the later transfer itself.

**Independent support:** Mexican constitutional provisions; INE and 2024 election records should be used for full succession verification.

**Relevant lenses:** 4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.8, 4B.10.

## 4. Information environment and evidence limitations

The political-freedom record was comparatively well documented, but several limitations remain:

- official statements are useful for laws, appointments, and stated intent but often present institutional weakening as cost reduction or efficiency;
- Plan B’s legal defect is clear from the Supreme Court, but the counterfactual effect on the 2024 election cannot be directly observed;
- executive pressure on INAI involved presidential rhetoric and Senate inaction, but personal command over the Senate’s failure to appoint commissioners is not proven;
- media-attack figures differ between Article 19, CPJ, RSF, and government sources because definitions and verification standards differ;
- surveillance allegations are strong at the institutional level but do not establish that López Obrador personally authorized Pegasus operations;
- political speech can create pressure and online harassment without proving direct legal or physical retaliation;
- federal and state responsibilities are difficult to separate, especially for journalist safety, protest policing, Indigenous activism, and local political violence.

## 5. Important questions for deeper research

1. What was the full text and operational effect of each Plan B provision before invalidation?
2. Did the administration seek any additional electoral or constitutional changes in 2023 that could affect succession or party entrenchment?
3. Which INAI nominees did López Obrador reject, on what legal basis, and how did Senate inaction affect pending cases?
4. What were the appointment histories and political affiliations of Supreme Court justices appointed during his presidency?
5. Did the executive use public advertising, tax enforcement, licensing, defamation law, or data disclosure to pressure critical outlets?
6. What legal authority and chain of command governed Pegasus surveillance, and were any officials held accountable?
7. How did presidential attacks on journalists correlate with online threats, physical attacks, or official harassment?
8. Did political parties and civil-society organizations have equal access to public resources and campaign opportunities?
9. What independent evidence exists about state-media concentration, government advertising, and editorial pressure in 2023?
10. Did the 2023 institutional checks leave Mexico’s democratic resilience stronger or weaker at the end of the period?

- [Freedom House, Mexico: Freedom on the Net 2023](https://freedomhouse.org/country/mexico/freedom-net/2023)
- [Reuters Institute, Digital News Report 2023: Mexico](https://reutersinstitute.politics.ox.ac.uk/digital-news-report/2023/mexico)
- [Committee to Protect Journalists, May 2023 report](https://cpj.org/es/2023/05/el-presidente-mexicano-andres-manuel-lopez-obrador-critica-a-medios-de-prensa-y-a-organizacion-defensora-de-la-libertad-de-expresion-por-la-cobertura-de-presuntos-operativos-de-espionaje-ilegales/)
- [El País, INAI institutional crisis](https://elpais.com/mexico/2023-03-16/lopez-obrador-deja-al-organismo-de-transparencia-al-borde-de-la-inoperancia.html)
- [Article 19, 2023 press-violence report](https://articulo19.org/informe-semestral-2023-violencia-contra-la-prensa-entre-ataques-estigmatizacion-y-ausencia-del-estado/)

## 7. Promising leads still needing inspection

- Full Supreme Court judgment on Plan B, including substantive electoral effects.
- Full SCJN judgment on INAI vacancies and the Senate’s constitutional obligations.
- INE reports on 2023 institutional capacity, budget reductions, and preparations for the 2024 election.
- Mexican constitutional and legislative records concerning presidential succession and term limits.
- Government advertising allocation data for 2023.
- Full Article 19 report on official disinformation and access to information.
- Citizen Lab forensic material and Mexican investigative files concerning Pegasus.
- Appointment and confirmation records for Supreme Court justices and autonomous-body officials.
- 2023 reports from CPJ, RSF, and Mexico’s journalist-protection mechanism.
- Election-observer and OAS/UN assessments of the 2024 election, for later retrospective evaluation of the institutional baseline created in 2023.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 5B ---

# Chapter 5B — Economic Well-Being and Prosperity

Period: 2023–2023  
Ruler: Andrés Manuel López Obrador, President of Mexico  
Prompt version: `chapter_research_prompt_v2`  
Lens presentation: `layered_lenses_v1`

## 1. Orientation

Mexico experienced a broad economic expansion in 2023. INEGI reported real GDP growth of approximately 3.2 percent, while the IMF described growth as broad-based and led by private consumption, investment, services, construction, and automobile production. Inflation declined substantially from its 2022 peak but remained above the central bank’s 3 percent target.

The administration pursued higher minimum wages, direct transfers, public infrastructure, energy sovereignty, and regional development. The 2023 minimum-wage increase was 20 percent. CONEVAL’s latest poverty measurement, released in 2023, showed poverty at 36.3 percent in 2022, down from 41.9 percent in 2018, although healthcare deprivation increased and the data did not measure 2023 conditions.

Macroeconomic management remained relatively stable, with contained debt and access to financial markets, but fiscal buffers were limited, the tax base remained weak, and public investment and regulatory constraints threatened longer-term growth. Nearshoring offered a major opportunity, but the IMF identified crime, corruption, low female labor-force participation, financing barriers, and regulatory obstacles as constraints.

The strongest evidence supports material income and minimum-wage gains alongside moderate growth and macroeconomic stability. It does not establish that the government created a substantially stronger long-term productivity trajectory, eliminated regional inequality, or fully converted public spending into durable productive capacity.

## 2. Disposition by lens

| Lens | Disposition | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| 5B.1 Broad and sustainable prosperity | Evidence of broad growth and redistribution policy; sustainability mixed | WEB-5B-001, WEB-5B-003, WEB-5B-006 |
| 5B.2 Qualified economic leadership | Partial evidence; further appointment and autonomy research needed | WEB-5B-002 |
| 5B.3 Macroeconomic stability | Evidence of relative stability, with structural fiscal weaknesses | WEB-5B-002, WEB-5B-005 |
| 5B.4 Fair economic rules | Mixed; labor gains but regulatory and competition constraints | WEB-5B-003, WEB-5B-006 |
| 5B.5 Resistance to economic capture | Incomplete and mixed evidence; corruption and rule-of-law concerns remained | WEB-5B-002, WEB-5B-006 |
| 5B.6 Productive public investment | Infrastructure and construction expanded, but quality, allocation, and opportunity-cost evidence remain incomplete | WEB-5B-001, WEB-5B-006 |
| 5B.7 Evidence and correction | Independent statistical and monetary institutions remained active; government claims sometimes exceeded available evidence | WEB-5B-002, WEB-5B-004 |
| 5B.8 Fair distribution | Minimum wages and transfers improved distributional outcomes; regional and service-access gaps persisted | WEB-5B-003, WEB-5B-004 |
| 5B.9 Managing economic shocks | Inflation response was broadly stable, but high rates and food-price pressures burdened households | WEB-5B-002, WEB-5B-004 |
| 5B.10 Lasting economic trajectory | Mixed evidence; stronger consumption and wages, unresolved productivity and fiscal-capacity constraints | WEB-5B-001, WEB-5B-002, WEB-5B-005, WEB-5B-006 |

## 3. Evidence records

### WEB-5B-001 — Broad real GDP growth in 2023

**Claim and significance:** INEGI reported that Mexico’s national GDP at market prices increased from 24.273 trillion pesos in 2022 to 25.073 trillion pesos in 2023, while real gross value added increased by 3.2 percent. Growth was broad but uneven across states: Quintana Roo, San Luis Potosí, Oaxaca, and Campeche grew strongly, while Coahuila and Tamaulipas contracted slightly.

**Strongest source:** *Producto Interno Bruto por Entidad Federativa (PIBE) 2023*, INEGI, 2024. [Direct source](https://www.inegi.org.mx/contenidos/saladeprensa/boletines/2024/PIBEF/PIBEF2023.pdf)

**Locator:** PDF p. 3, national and state GDP table.

**Period status:** Target-year outcome evidence.

**Ruler attribution:** Weak to moderate. GDP was affected by global recovery, U.S. demand, private investment, remittances, and nearshoring, as well as domestic policy.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** Growth was positive but not exceptional for Mexico’s development needs, and state-level outcomes were uneven.

**Source cautions:** GDP does not measure distribution, informal work, household welfare, environmental costs, or policy causation.

**Independent support:** IMF, *Mexico: 2023 Article IV Consultation*; Banco de México.

**Relevant lenses:** 5B.1, 5B.6, 5B.9, 5B.10.

---

### WEB-5B-002 — Macroeconomic stability with unresolved fiscal and structural weaknesses

**Claim and significance:** The IMF described Mexico’s 2023 economy as undergoing broad-based expansion, with projected 3.2 percent growth. It also recommended reforms to improve female labor participation, tackle crime and corruption, expand access to finance, reduce formalization costs, remove regulatory barriers, increase renewable-energy supply, and strengthen competition. OECD analysis identified weak fiscal buffers and an incomplete fiscal-rule framework.

**Strongest source:** *Mexico: 2023 Article IV Consultation—Press Release and Staff Report*, IMF, November 1, 2023. [Direct source](https://www.imf.org/en/publications/cr/issues/2023/10/31/mexico-2023-article-iv-consultation-press-release-and-staff-report-541023)

**Locator:** IMF summary, paras. describing 2023 expansion and recommended structural reforms.

**Corroborating source:** *Macroeconomic developments and policy challenges*, OECD Economic Surveys: Mexico 2024. [Direct source](https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/oecd-economic-surveys-mexico-2024_b8d974db-en/full-report/component-5.html)

**Locator:** Paras. 4392–4395, describing fiscal buffers, fiscal rules, and the need for a stronger debt anchor.

**Period status:** Target-year assessment and ruler-period structural context.

**Ruler attribution:** Moderate to strong for fiscal and regulatory policy, but monetary policy was conducted independently by Banco de México.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** Mexico maintained a record of meeting fiscal targets, relatively contained public debt, and access to international finance. The IMF described risks as broadly balanced.

**Source cautions:** IMF and OECD recommendations are institutional assessments rather than direct measurements of household welfare or political motivation.

**Relevant lenses:** 5B.1, 5B.2, 5B.3, 5B.4, 5B.5, 5B.7, 5B.10.

---

### WEB-5B-003 — Minimum wage increased by 20 percent

**Claim and significance:** Mexico’s statutory minimum wage increased by 20 percent from January 1, 2023, to 207.44 pesos per day in most of the country and 312.41 pesos in the northern border zone. The labor ministry described the increase as a major recovery of purchasing power.

**Strongest source:** *Entran en vigor salarios mínimos 2023 en todo el país*, Mexican Secretariat of Labor and Social Welfare, January 1, 2023. [Direct source](https://www.gob.mx/stps/prensa/entran-en-vigor-salarios-minimos-2023-en-todo-el-pais?idiom=es)

**Locator:** Communiqué stating the 20 percent increase, monthly increase, and estimated real purchasing-power recovery.

**Period status:** Target-year policy evidence.

**Ruler attribution:** Strong. López Obrador publicly announced and politically promoted the increase, which was adopted through the National Minimum Wage Commission.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** Minimum-wage workers remained low-paid by international standards, many workers were informal or outside direct coverage, and food prices threatened to erode gains.

**Source cautions:** The government’s estimate of a 90 percent purchasing-power recovery is a policy claim; independent real-wage calculations should be used for final assessment.

**Independent support:** OECD Employment Outlook 2023, which reported that Mexico’s real minimum wage rose 43.6 percent from December 2020 to May 2023, the largest increase in the OECD. [OECD](https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/oecd-employment-outlook-2023_08785bba-en/full-report/mexico_f53d8013.html)

**Relevant lenses:** 5B.1, 5B.4, 5B.8, 5B.9.

---

### WEB-5B-004 — Poverty declined, but 2023 publication measured 2022 conditions

**Claim and significance:** CONEVAL’s multidimensional poverty measurement released in 2023 found that 36.3 percent of Mexico’s population was in poverty in 2022, compared with 41.9 percent in 2018. The same measurement found significant social deprivations, including 50.4 percent lacking access to social security and 39.0 percent lacking access to health services. Without social transfers, the poverty rate would have been 39.0 percent rather than 36.3 percent.

**Strongest source:** *Medición multidimensional de la pobreza en México 2022*, CONEVAL, August 2023. [Direct source](https://www.coneval.org.mx/Medicion/MP/Documents/MMP_2022/Pobreza_multidimensional_2022.pdf)

**Locator:** PDF pp. 17–22, 26, and 34; especially pp. 34, 36.3 percent poverty and transfer effects.

**Period status:** Published during 2023 but measures 2022 conditions.

**Ruler attribution:** Moderate. Transfers, wage policy, labor-market recovery, and economic growth were relevant, but poverty also reflected preexisting conditions, post-pandemic recovery, remittances, and state-level policy.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** Poverty fell substantially from 2018, and transfers reduced measured poverty. Healthcare access worsened, and high regional disparities persisted.

**Source cautions:** The measurement cannot directly evaluate 2023 policy implementation. The causal effect of any single government program is not established by the aggregate poverty statistic.

**Independent support:** CONEVAL’s methodology page; World Bank and BBVA Research summaries.

**Relevant lenses:** 5B.1, 5B.7, 5B.8, 5B.10.

---

### WEB-5B-005 — Inflation declined but remained above target and constrained households

**Claim and significance:** Banco de México reported that headline inflation declined during 2023, reaching approximately 4.66 percent for the year, while the policy interest rate remained high at 11.25 percent through most of the period. By the fourth quarter, general inflation was approximately 4.41 percent. The combination of falling inflation and high interest rates supported macroeconomic stability but constrained credit and investment.

**Strongest source:** *Reporte de Estabilidad Financiera* and Banco de México’s 2023 quarterly reporting. [Direct source](https://www.banxico.org.mx/TablasWeb/reporte-estabilidad-financiera/2-REF-2023-2S-CM/0AAB90A4-81E1-4D4C-A1DD-8D92AC07070C.html)

**Locator:** Table listing 2023 general inflation at 4.66 percent and core inflation at 4.44 percent.

**Corroborating source:** *Informe trimestral octubre–diciembre 2023*, Banco de México. [Direct source](https://www.banxico.org.mx/publicaciones-y-prensa/resumenes-visuales/informe-trimestral-html/informe-trimestral-oct-dic-00002.html)

**Period status:** Target-year outcome and policy context.

**Ruler attribution:** Weak to moderate. Banco de México’s monetary decisions were institutionally independent; fiscal, energy, wage, and market policies were more directly attributable to the executive.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** Inflation fell substantially from 2022 and financial stability was maintained. High rates also reflected the central bank’s effort to bring inflation back toward its target.

**Source cautions:** Inflation averages conceal higher food and housing burdens for poorer households and do not by themselves measure living standards.

**Relevant lenses:** 5B.3, 5B.7, 5B.8, 5B.9, 5B.10.

---

### WEB-5B-006 — Nearshoring opportunity was real but constrained by crime, corruption, regulation, and low female participation

**Claim and significance:** The IMF identified supply-chain diversification and Mexico’s proximity to the United States as potential drivers of investment, employment, and growth. It also warned that Mexico needed to address crime, corruption, rule of law, low female labor-force participation, access to finance, and regulatory barriers to capture those gains.

**Strongest source:** *Mexico: 2023 Article IV Consultation—Press Release and Staff Report*, IMF, November 1, 2023. [Direct source](https://www.imf.org/en/publications/cr/issues/2023/10/31/mexico-2023-article-iv-consultation-press-release-and-staff-report-541023)

**Locator:** IMF summary, paragraphs describing supply-chain diversification, nearshoring, female labor participation, crime, corruption, finance, and regulation.

**Period status:** Target-year assessment of an opportunity with longer-term implications.

**Ruler attribution:** Moderate. The external opportunity was not created by López Obrador, but domestic security, energy, regulatory, competition, and infrastructure policies affected its realization.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** Private investment rose strongly in parts of 2023, and Mexico benefited from manufacturing relocation and U.S. demand. The report does not establish that government policy caused missed investment.

**Source cautions:** Nearshoring projections are inherently uncertain; announced investment does not equal completed capital formation or broad household gains.

**Independent support:** OECD economic surveys; Mexican foreign-investment data; private-sector reporting.

**Relevant lenses:** 5B.1, 5B.3, 5B.4, 5B.5, 5B.6, 5B.10.

---

### WEB-5B-007 — Economic growth and public investment were regionally uneven

**Claim and significance:** INEGI’s 2023 state GDP data showed strong growth in Quintana Roo, San Luis Potosí, Oaxaca, Campeche, and Yucatán, while several other states grew slowly or contracted. This indicates that national growth did not translate uniformly across regions.

**Strongest source:** *Producto Interno Bruto por Entidad Federativa (PIBE) 2023*, INEGI, 2024. [Direct source](https://www.inegi.org.mx/contenidos/saladeprensa/boletines/2024/PIBEF/PIBEF2023.pdf)

**Locator:** PDF p. 3, state-level GDP table.

**Period status:** Target-year outcome evidence.

**Ruler attribution:** Weak to moderate. Regional outcomes reflected tourism, manufacturing, energy, federal infrastructure, private investment, and state-level conditions.

**Contrary or complicating evidence:** Some southern states, including Oaxaca and Campeche, recorded strong growth; the data do not show uniform regional neglect.

**Source cautions:** State GDP measures production, not household income, poverty, service quality, or distribution within states.

**Relevant lenses:** 5B.1, 5B.6, 5B.8, 5B.10.

## 4. Information environment and evidence limitations

The economic record was more reliable than the political or security record because INEGI, CONEVAL, Banco de México, IMF, OECD, and labor institutions published standardized data. The principal limitations were:

- CONEVAL’s 2023 poverty release measured 2022, not 2023;
- GDP growth does not establish household prosperity or policy causation;
- headline inflation understates the burden on households facing higher food, rent, and healthcare costs;
- official wage-recovery claims require comparison with real wages, informal employment, hours, and regional prices;
- nearshoring figures often refer to announced investment rather than completed projects;
- public-investment quality, cost overruns, procurement, and opportunity costs require project-level audits;
- Mexico’s large informal sector makes formal employment and minimum-wage indicators incomplete;
- macroeconomic stability was partly the result of independent central-bank policy and external conditions, not solely presidential decisions.

## 5. Important questions for deeper research

1. What was the full-year 2023 change in median real household income, real wages, and consumption?
2. How much of the poverty decline was attributable to transfers, wages, remittances, post-pandemic recovery, and employment?
3. Did healthcare deprivation continue worsening in 2023 after the transition from Seguro Popular to INSABI and IMSS-Bienestar?
4. What were the 2023 federal tax collection, deficit, debt-service, and public-investment execution figures?
5. Did major infrastructure projects deliver measurable productivity, regional, tourism, or employment gains relative to their costs?
6. Which economic officials retained operational independence, and how were technical appointments made?
7. Did public procurement and state-owned enterprises favor politically connected contractors or military-linked firms?
8. What share of announced nearshoring investment was actually completed in 2023?
9. How did inflation affect poor households differently from higher-income households?
10. Did minimum-wage increases produce meaningful gains after accounting for informality, food prices, and regional living costs?
11. Were gains distributed across women, Indigenous communities, southern states, and rural households?
12. What fiscal reforms were proposed or rejected to strengthen Mexico’s long-term capacity to respond to shocks?

## 6. Corroborating sources

Opened or examined mainly for corroboration:

- [IMF, Mexico 2023 Article IV Consultation](https://www.imf.org/en/publications/cr/issues/2023/10/31/mexico-2023-article-iv-consultation-press-release-and-staff-report-541023)
- [OECD Employment Outlook 2023: Mexico](https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/oecd-employment-outlook-2023_08785bba-en/full-report/mexico_f53d8013.html)
- [OECD Economic Surveys: Mexico 2024](https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/oecd-economic-surveys-mexico-2024_b8d974db-en/full-report/component-5.html)
- [CONEVAL poverty measurement 2022](https://www.coneval.org.mx/Medicion/MP/Documents/MMP_2022/Pobreza_multidimensional_2022.pdf)
- [INEGI state GDP 2023](https://www.inegi.org.mx/contenidos/saladeprensa/boletines/2024/PIBEF/PIBEF2023.pdf)
- [Banco de México 2023 quarterly report](https://www.banxico.org.mx/publicaciones-y-prensa/resumenes-visuales/informe-trimestral-html/informe-trimestral-oct-dic-00002.html)
- [Mexico 2023 minimum wage decree](https://sidof.segob.gob.mx/notas/docFuente/5673550)
- [Mexican labor ministry 2023 wage announcement](https://www.gob.mx/stps/prensa/entran-en-vigor-salarios-minimos-2023-en-todo-el-pais?idiom=es)

## 7. Promising leads still needing inspection

- INEGI 2023 ENIGH or household-income releases that directly measure 2023 living standards.
- CONEVAL quarterly labor-poverty series for all four quarters of 2023.
- 2023 federal budget execution, public-investment, procurement, and audit records.
- Project-level audits for Tren Maya, Dos Bocas, AIFA, and other major infrastructure programs.
- 2023 tax collection and debt-service data from SHCP.
- Banco de México’s full 2023 inflation and household-expectations tables.
- Formal employment, informal employment, and wage-distribution data by gender and region.
- Mexico’s 2023 foreign-direct-investment records separating reinvestment, mergers, and new nearshoring investment.
- IMF and OECD detailed estimates of female labor-force participation, regulatory constraints, and productivity.
- Independent evaluations of social-transfer targeting, leakage, and effects on poverty and inequality.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 6B ---

# 6B — Social Well-Being and Human Development

## 1. Orientation

Andrés Manuel López Obrador was Mexico’s constitutional president throughout 2023. The federal executive controlled major cash-transfer programs, federal health policy, national education policy, disaster coordination, and substantial public investment. His practical influence was strongest over federal programs and budgets, but service delivery also depended on states, municipalities, autonomous agencies, courts, Congress, and implementation capacity.

The main evidence problem is temporal. Mexico’s most comprehensive poverty and deprivation results released in 2023 describe household conditions in 2022, not the full 2023 year. They are therefore useful for inherited conditions and policy trajectory, but cannot alone establish end-2023 outcomes.

The evidence indicates real welfare gains by 2022, including reduced monetary poverty and child poverty, but persistent and severe inequalities in health access, social security, indigenous and rural poverty, and regional development. In 2023, the government continued social-program and health-system reforms while confronting implementation uncertainty and Hurricane Otis. Independent evidence is stronger for outcomes and constraints than for whether federal social programs were administered impartially, professionally, or with effective correction.

## 2. Evidence index

### Record WEB-6B-001 — Poverty fell before the target year, but the 2023 release measured 2022

CONEVAL reported that Mexico’s 2022 poverty rate was 36.3%, representing 46.8 million people; extreme poverty was 7.1%, or 9.1 million people. The figures were based on the 2022 ENIGH household survey and were released in 2023. This is important evidence of the social conditions inherited into 2023, but not a direct measure of conditions during the whole target year.

CONEVAL also estimated that poverty was 36.3% when social transfers were included, compared with 39.0% in a counterfactual excluding transfers; extreme poverty was 7.1% with transfers and 8.9% without them. This supports an association between transfers and lower measured poverty, but does not establish that AMLO’s particular programs caused the full difference.

- Period: inherited/2022 condition reported in 2023.
- Attribution: moderate. Federal transfers were a major presidential policy, but poverty outcomes also reflected employment, wages, remittances, inflation, state programs, and broader economic conditions.
- Caution: CONEVAL’s counterfactual is a statistical comparison, not a randomized evaluation.

Source: CONEVAL, *Medición de la pobreza multidimensional en México 2022*, August 2023, pp. 17 and 34. [PDF](https://www.coneval.org.mx/Medicion/MP/Documents/MMP_2022/Pobreza_multidimensional_2022.pdf)

### Record WEB-6B-002 — Poverty remained sharply unequal across groups and regions

CONEVAL’s 2022 results showed substantial disparities: poverty affected 65.2% of Indigenous people compared with 33.1% of non-Indigenous people; 48.8% of rural residents compared with 32.2% of urban residents; and 41.2% of people with disabilities compared with 35.9% of people without disabilities. Child poverty was 48.1% among children aged five or younger, 47.0% among children aged six to eleven, and 42.9% among adolescents aged twelve to seventeen. Poverty was also much higher among women aged sixty-five or older than among men of the same age.

State-level disparities were similarly large: poverty affected 67.4% of residents in Chiapas, 60.4% in Guerrero, and 58.4% in Oaxaca, compared with 13.3% in Baja California Sur.

These results matter because national averages conceal persistent unequal life chances. They also limit claims that social gains were broadly distributed by the end of 2023.

- Period: inherited/2022 condition.
- Attribution: moderate to weak for outcomes; strong for the federal government’s responsibility to target and coordinate social policy, but state governments and structural conditions remained important.
- Caution: these are cross-sectional poverty measurements, not evaluations of individual programs.

Source: CONEVAL, *Medición de la pobreza multidimensional en México 2022*, August 2023, pp. 31–37. [PDF](https://www.coneval.org.mx/Medicion/MP/Documents/MMP_2022/Pobreza_multidimensional_2022.pdf)

### Record WEB-6B-003 — Social-security exclusion remained a major structural deficit

CONEVAL reported that 63.6% of employed people lacked direct access to social security and that 50.7% of people outside employment also lacked such access. This is central to health access, pensions, income security, and protection against illness or job loss. It indicates that poverty reduction coexisted with a large formal-institutional coverage gap.

- Period: inherited/2022 condition reported in 2023.
- Attribution: moderate. Federal labor, health, and social-protection policy influenced the problem, but informality, employer practices, state implementation, and long-term structural conditions also mattered.
- Caution: “lack of access” is a multidimensional measure and should not be treated as identical to complete absence of all health or welfare services.

Source: CONEVAL, *Medición de la pobreza multidimensional en México 2022*, August 2023, p. 26. [PDF](https://www.coneval.org.mx/Medicion/MP/Documents/MMP_2022/Pobreza_multidimensional_2022.pdf)

### Record WEB-6B-004 — Health-system reform created significant implementation uncertainty

CONEVAL’s 2023 study on the right to health examined access for people without social-security coverage in the context of reforms to the General Health Law, the transition from INSABI, and federalization of state health services. The study’s framing establishes that access for uninsured people and institutional transition were active policy problems during the target period.

This matters because formal expansion of health coverage cannot be equated with effective, affordable, and continuous care. The source supports the existence and importance of the reform challenge, but the webpage summary does not establish whether access improved or deteriorated for all groups in 2023.

- Period: target-year policy and implementation context.
- Attribution: strong for the reform agenda, since these were federal policy changes; weaker for local delivery outcomes.
- Caution: the webpage is a summary; the underlying study requires further extraction before using detailed findings.

Source: CONEVAL, *Estudio sobre el derecho a la salud 2023: un análisis cualitativo*, August 2023. [Source page](https://www.coneval.org.mx/InformesPublicaciones/Paginas/Mosaicos/Analisis_cualitativo_salud_2023.aspx)

### Record WEB-6B-005 — Child poverty improved but remained higher than general poverty

UNICEF reported that child and adolescent poverty fell from 52.6% in 2020 to 45.8% in 2022, while also emphasizing that poverty remained more entrenched among children and adolescents than in the population overall. UNICEF’s 2023 work focused on child health, education, protection, and social programs.

The reduction is favorable evidence for the broader welfare trajectory, but it predates the target year and cannot be assigned solely to the federal executive. Persistent child poverty indicates that transfer and social-policy gains did not eliminate unequal life chances.

- Period: inherited/2022 outcome reported in a 2023 report.
- Attribution: moderate; federal social programs were relevant, but household labor income, state services, demographics, and economic recovery also mattered.
- Caution: UNICEF’s report is an institutional overview and does not independently evaluate the causal effect of each Mexican program.

Source: UNICEF Mexico, *Informe anual 2023*, 2023 report. [Report page](https://www.unicef.org/mexico/informes/informeanual2023)

### Record WEB-6B-006 — The government and partners responded to Hurricane Otis, but evidence is limited on adequacy and distribution

UNICEF reported that, after Hurricane Otis, its 2023 response supported federal, state, and municipal authorities and benefited 32,727 people. The evidence documents an operational response and external assistance, not the full adequacy, equity, speed, or durability of the government’s disaster response.

This is relevant to crisis protection and service continuity, especially for children and displaced or otherwise vulnerable residents. It should not be treated as proof that the federal response was sufficient.

- Period: target year.
- Attribution: moderate. The response involved federal, state, municipal, and international actors.
- Caution: UNICEF’s reported beneficiary figure is an output measure, not an independent estimate of all affected people or unmet need.

Source: UNICEF Mexico, *Informe anual 2023*, 2023 report. [Report page](https://www.unicef.org/mexico/informes/informeanual2023)

### Record WEB-6B-007 — Labor poverty remained substantial despite rising real labor income

El País reported, citing CONEVAL’s labor-poverty measure, that labor poverty was 37.8% in the second quarter of 2023, essentially unchanged from 37.7% a year earlier. Real labor income per person rose 6.8% year-on-year, but more than one million people entered the labor market and 51.4% of workers were informal.

This complicates a simple prosperity narrative: real labor income improved, yet a large share of households still could not purchase the basic food basket from labor income alone. The evidence is relevant to social protection and living standards, although broader economic distribution belongs partly in Chapter 5B.

- Period: target year, second quarter of 2023.
- Attribution: weak to moderate. Federal labor and social policy mattered, but labor-market outcomes were also shaped by private employers, state economies, inflation, and post-pandemic recovery.
- Caution: this is high-quality reporting of a CONEVAL indicator, not the underlying CONEVAL publication itself.

Source: *La pobreza laboral en México se estanca: a 49 millones de personas no les alcanza para la canasta básica*, El País México, 29 August 2023. [Article](https://elpais.com/mexico/2023-08-29/la-pobreza-laboral-en-mexico-se-estanca-a-49-millones-de-personas-no-les-alcanza-para-la-canasta-basica.html)

## 3. Lens dispositions

- **6B.1 — Welfare as a governing purpose:** Partially evidenced. Poverty reduction, transfers, child programs, and health reform show welfare as a stated and funded priority. Independent evidence is insufficient to determine how far this exceeded political messaging or patronage.
- **6B.2 — Access to essential services:** Incomplete. CONEVAL documents health-system transition and social-security exclusion, but a complete 2023 assessment of effective health, education, water, housing, and sanitation access remains open.
- **6B.3 — Priority for vulnerable groups:** Supported but qualified. Child, Indigenous, rural, disability, and regional disparities remained pronounced.
- **6B.4 — Professional service delivery:** Major gap. The opened sources do not adequately establish staffing quality, procurement integrity, administrative competence, or politicization.
- **6B.5 — Measurement and correction:** Mixed. CONEVAL and UNICEF provide useful measurement, but evidence on whether AMLO’s government systematically corrected failed programs is insufficient.
- **6B.6 — Protection during crises:** Partially evidenced. The Otis response is documented, but adequacy, coverage, and distribution require deeper examination.
- **6B.7 — No political allocation of welfare:** Unresolved. No sufficiently strong opened source establishes either systematic political conditionality or its absence.
- **6B.8 — Dignity and equal opportunity:** Partially adverse. Persistent Indigenous, rural, disability, child, and regional disparities show unequal opportunity; direct evidence on enforcement and remedy is incomplete.
- **6B.9 — Durable social institutions:** Unresolved/mixed. Health-system restructuring created institutional change, but durability, professionalization, and post-presidential resilience cannot yet be established.
- **6B.10 — Lasting life chances:** Mixed and temporally limited. Poverty and child-poverty measures improved through 2022, while severe social-security exclusion and group disparities persisted. Full end-2023 evidence is incomplete.

## 4. Information environment and limitations

Reliable measurement was possible through CONEVAL, UNICEF, and labor-market statistics, but several limitations matter:

- The principal poverty evidence released in 2023 measured 2022 conditions.
- National averages conceal large Indigenous, rural, disability, child, gender, and state-level differences.
- Poverty and transfer comparisons are observational and do not isolate presidential policy effects.
- Health-system transition made coverage definitions and institutional responsibility difficult to compare over time.
- Disaster-response figures generally measure activities or beneficiaries, not unmet need, quality, or long-term recovery.
- Federal responsibility is difficult to separate from state and municipal administration.
- Government reporting and politically polarized public debate create incentives to emphasize either social-program expansion or implementation failure.

## 5. Questions for deeper research

1. What did 2023 administrative and household data show about actual health-service use, waiting times, medicine shortages, and out-of-pocket costs?
2. How did the INSABI-to-IMSS-Bienestar transition affect uninsured people in different states?
3. Did federal transfers reach recipients through neutral eligibility rules, or were there documented partisan or electoral effects?
4. What were the 2023 education attendance, learning, dropout, and school-infrastructure outcomes?
5. How much of the poverty decline came from transfers, wages, remittances, inflation changes, and post-pandemic recovery?
6. What independent audits evaluated flagship social programs, disaster spending, and procurement?
7. Did Hurricane Otis recovery resources reach poorer, rural, Indigenous, and displaced residents equitably?
8. Which welfare institutions acquired statutory, professional, and financial durability beyond AMLO’s presidency?

## 6. Source groups

### Evidence opened and used

1. CONEVAL, *Medición de la pobreza multidimensional en México 2022*.
2. CONEVAL, *Estudio sobre el derecho a la salud 2023: un análisis cualitativo*.
3. UNICEF Mexico, *Informe anual 2023*.
4. El País México report on 2023 labor poverty.

### Opened and useful mainly for corroboration

- UNICEF’s framing of child health, education, protection, social programs, and post-Otis support corroborates the crisis-response and vulnerable-groups records.
- CONEVAL’s health-study page corroborates that institutional transition and uninsured access were central 2023 policy issues.

### Promising leads still requiring inspection

- WHO/European Observatory, *Health Systems in Action 2024: Mexico*: [link](https://eurohealthobservatory.who.int/publications/i/health-systems-in-action-2024-mexico)
- UNICEF Mexico 2023 country-office annual report PDF: [link](https://www.unicef.org/media/152426/file/Mexico-2023-COAR.pdf)
- IMSS, *Memoria Estadística 2023*: [link](https://www.imss.gob.mx/conoce-al-imss/memoria-estadistica-2023)
- Official Hurricane Otis recovery reports from Mexico’s federal authorities.
- CONEVAL and INEGI 2023 labor, health, education, and household microdata.
- ASF audits of health reform, social transfers, and disaster-recovery expenditure.
- Independent evaluations of Becas Benito Juárez, Pensión para el Bienestar, Sembrando Vida, and IMSS-Bienestar.

Source count: 7 developed records; 4 principal opened sources; several corroborating and lead sources remain to be inspected. No chapter score assigned.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 7B ---

# 7B — Ruler Personal Integrity and Honesty

## 1. Orientation

Andrés Manuel López Obrador was Mexico’s president throughout 2023 and exercised strong control over the federal executive, public messaging, appointments, and major programs. His formal anti-corruption platform emphasized austerity, personal frugality, and ending “neoliberal” corruption. His practical accountability environment was constrained by partisan legislative control, incomplete transparency institutions, military opacity, and dependence on independent courts, auditors, journalists, and watchdogs.

The strongest evidence does not establish that López Obrador personally received illicit financial benefit. It does establish several integrity risks: serious irregularities in Segalmex, efforts that weakened or obstructed the operation of INAI, repeated attacks on accountability institutions and journalists, and the president’s public disclosure of a New York Times reporter’s phone number in 2024. Personal and family conflict-of-interest allegations remained disputed and should not be treated as proven findings without stronger underlying records.

## 2. Evidence records

### WEB-7B-001 — Segalmex generated major unresolved public-fund irregularities

The Superior Audit Office reported irregularities totaling 15.151 billion pesos in Segalmex during its first three years, including 2.346 billion pesos connected to 2021. Reported problems included undocumented expenditures, questionable contracts, missing goods, unverified producer incentives, and suspected diversion. The matter was subject to Fiscalía investigations, and at least one former finance director was detained.

This matters because Segalmex was created by López Obrador’s government and was a flagship food-security institution. The evidence supports serious institutional failure and accountability risk, but not personal enrichment by López Obrador.

- Period: target-year accountability of inherited 2019–2021 conduct.
- Attribution: strong for creation, supervision, and political responsibility; weak for personal participation or profit.
- Contrary evidence: the government pursued criminal complaints and recovery efforts; audit irregularities are not equivalent to final judicial findings of theft.

Source: *Segalmex, el hoyo negro de los fondos públicos de López Obrador*, El País México, 22 February 2023, reporting Superior Audit findings. [Source](https://elpais.com/mexico/2023-02-22/segalmex-el-hoyo-negro-de-los-fondos-publicos-de-lopez-obrador.html)

### WEB-7B-002 — INAI was left unable to operate normally after appointments were blocked

Human Rights Watch reported that López Obrador vetoed Senate-approved appointments to Mexico’s transparency agency, INAI, while the Senate failed to fill vacancies. By March 2023 INAI had only four of the five commissioners needed to meet and issue decisions, leaving 3,947 cases pending as of May. Human Rights Watch also documented López Obrador’s repeated proposals to eliminate INAI or transfer its functions to executive-linked auditing bodies.

This is direct evidence relevant to obstruction and institutional accountability. It does not prove that the president sought to conceal a particular personal wrongdoing, but it weakened an institution capable of investigating government secrecy and corruption.

- Period: target year.
- Attribution: strong for the presidential veto and public position; shared with the Morena-controlled Senate.
- Contrary evidence: López Obrador argued that INAI was ineffective and that other state institutions could perform its functions.

Source: *Mexico: Public Accountability, Privacy Under Threat*, Human Rights Watch, 24 May 2023. [Source](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/05/24/mexico-public-accountability-privacy-under-threat)

### WEB-7B-003 — Transparency complaints increased during López Obrador’s administration

El País reported that, during the first four years of López Obrador’s government, 77,715 challenges were filed against authorities’ responses to information requests, compared with 31,786 during the comparable period under the previous administration. The share of requests generating complaints rose from 5.6% in 2019 to 6.9% in 2022. The article linked the deterioration to the president’s sustained criticism of INAI and the failure to appoint commissioners.

This is evidence of a broader transparency problem, but it is not by itself proof of intentional concealment or personal dishonesty. More requests may also reflect increased public scrutiny.

- Period: broader ruler-period, with conditions continuing into 2023.
- Attribution: moderate; presidential rhetoric and appointments were relevant, but the figures cover all Mexican authorities.
- Contrary evidence: increased complaints may partly reflect more active use of the transparency system.

Source: *INAI: Las quejas por falta de transparencia se disparan durante el Gobierno de López Obrador*, El País México, 24 April 2023. [Source](https://elpais.com/mexico/2023-04-24/los-quejas-por-falta-de-transparencia-se-disparan-durante-el-gobierno-de-lopez-obrador.html)

### WEB-7B-004 — The president used hostile rhetoric toward independent accountability institutions

In 2023, López Obrador repeatedly described INAI as unnecessary or ineffective and supported proposals to eliminate it. Human Rights Watch documented that transferring INAI’s powers to executive-linked bodies would reduce independent scrutiny because those bodies reported to the political leadership they would be expected to oversee.

This matters to ethical example and public trust: the president’s stated anti-corruption agenda coexisted with hostility toward an institution designed to expose public-sector wrongdoing.

- Period: target year.
- Attribution: strong for the president’s public rhetoric and policy position.
- Contrary evidence: the administration argued that INAI had failed to prevent major corruption and that its functions could be performed more efficiently elsewhere.
- Caution: criticism of an institution is not itself proof of corruption or dishonesty.

Source: Human Rights Watch, *Mexico: Public Accountability, Privacy Under Threat*, 24 May 2023. [Source](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/05/24/mexico-public-accountability-privacy-under-threat)

### WEB-7B-005 — Later evidence documented the president publicly exposing a journalist’s phone number

In February 2024, López Obrador publicly read and displayed the phone number of a New York Times correspondent after the newspaper reported allegations concerning an abandoned US inquiry into contacts between people close to him and drug traffickers. The allegations were not independently established, no formal investigation of López Obrador was opened, and he denied them. INAI announced that it would investigate the disclosure of the journalist’s personal data.

This is later retrospective evidence concerning personal conduct and response to adverse reporting. It does not prove the underlying drug-financing allegations. It does provide evidence that the president responded to damaging reporting by publicly exposing personal information and attacking the reporter.

- Period: later retrospective evidence; event occurred in 2024, outside the target year.
- Attribution: strong for the public disclosure and denial.
- Contrary evidence: AP reported that the US inquiry was shelved, relied on unidentified officials and informants, and lacked known independent confirmation.
- Caution: this record should not be used as direct 2023 evidence except for broader patterns of rhetoric and accountability.

Source: *Mexico president denies report of allegations that close associates took drug money during campaign*, Associated Press, 22 February 2024. [Source](https://apnews.com/article/mexico-lopez-obrador-drug-money-campaign-7c16954987a391bd7c0f4c0f41dd5e579)

## 3. Lens dispositions

- **7B.1 — Truthfulness:** Mixed and unresolved. The record contains verifiable presidential statements and denials, but a systematic sample of false or accurate claims was not completed.
- **7B.2 — Correcting falsehoods and errors:** Adverse evidence exists regarding repeated attacks on INAI and refusal to accept the institution’s importance; evidence of corrections and remedies is incomplete.
- **7B.3 — Conflicts of interest:** Unresolved. Family and associate allegations require underlying procurement, ownership, and beneficial-ownership records.
- **7B.4 — Personal profit from office:** No accepted evidence establishes personal illicit enrichment by López Obrador in 2023. Segalmex establishes institutional financial irregularities, not personal profit.
- **7B.5 — Nepotism and loyalist appointments:** Incomplete. The INAI appointment dispute shows politicized institutional consequences, but a complete appointment-by-appointment competence and loyalty analysis remains outstanding.
- **7B.6 — Independent investigation:** Adverse evidence. INAI was left unable to operate normally, while courts and the ASF remained important counterweights.
- **7B.7 — Obstruction and concealment:** Partially supported. Blocking INAI’s functioning and classifying infrastructure information created accountability risks; direct proof of personal concealment is not established.
- **7B.8 — Promises and good faith:** Mixed. The anti-corruption and transparency platform conflicted with hostility toward INAI and weak transparency performance.
- **7B.9 — Favoritism and clientelism:** Unresolved to mixed. Segalmex and procurement allegations require deeper legal and audit review; no personal-benefit finding is established.
- **7B.10 — Ethical example and public trust:** Mixed/adverse evidence. Personal austerity rhetoric and anti-corruption commitments coexisted with institutional weakening, aggressive treatment of critics, and unresolved corruption scandals.

## 4. Information environment and limitations

Reliable evidence was available from the ASF, courts, INAI, Human Rights Watch, and investigative journalism. The main limitations were:

- audit irregularities were often unresolved administrative findings rather than final criminal judgments;
- allegations concerning family members, associates, and campaign finance were politically polarized and frequently lacked complete underlying records;
- executive rhetoric was extensive, but systematic verification of factual claims was difficult;
- weakening or paralysis of INAI reduced access to records needed to test conflicts and procurement;
- national corruption indices cannot establish personal conduct;
- personal enrichment should not be inferred from institutional corruption occurring under a ruler’s administration.

## 5. Questions for deeper research

1. What were the final ASF findings, Fiscalía actions, recoveries, and judgments in Segalmex by the end of 2023?
2. Did López Obrador or his close family have beneficial ownership, gifts, contracts, or financial ties connected to federal suppliers?
3. What formal legal basis supported his vetoes of INAI appointments?
4. How often did presidential statements in 2023 conflict with court rulings, audit findings, or official datasets?
5. Were officials implicated in corruption dismissed, prosecuted, protected, or reassigned?
6. Did procurement and contracting rules change in practice for military-led projects and flagship programs?
7. What records were classified under “national security,” and how often did courts or INAI reject those classifications?
8. Did anti-corruption reforms strengthen independent institutions or increase executive control?

## 6. Source groups

### Evidence opened and used

1. Human Rights Watch, *Mexico: Public Accountability, Privacy Under Threat*, 24 May 2023.
2. El País México, *INAI: Las quejas por falta de transparencia...*, 24 April 2023.
3. El País México, *Segalmex, el hoyo negro...*, 22 February 2023.
4. Associated Press, later retrospective report, 22 February 2024.

### Corroborating sources opened or identified

- ASF audit portal and 2023 audit-result pages: [ASF](https://www.asf.gob.mx/Trans/Informes/IR2023c/index.html)
- Presidential Segalmex transparency statement: [Presidencia](https://amlo.presidente.gob.mx/presidente-instruye-transparencia-total-en-caso-segalmex-gobierno-de-mexico-recupera-montos-y-mantiene-denuncias/)

### Promising leads still requiring inspection

- Detailed ASF Segalmex audit reports.
- Fiscalía General de la República case records and judicial resolutions.
- Public-declaration and procurement databases concerning the president’s family and associates.
- INAI resolutions and Supreme Court records on infrastructure secrecy.
- Mexican investigative reporting by MCCI, Animal Político, Proceso, and Latinus, with underlying documents independently checked.
- Presidential press-conference transcripts for a systematic truthfulness sample.

Source count: 5 developed records; 4 principal sources opened and used; 2 corroborating official sources identified; several underlying audit, legal, and financial records remain to be inspected. No score assigned.
