--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 8B ---

# 8B — Ruler Effectiveness and Competence

## 1. Orientation

Andrés Manuel López Obrador was Mexico’s president throughout 2023. His governing program was comparatively clear: a “Fourth Transformation” centered on social transfers, state-led infrastructure, energy sovereignty, austerity, anti-corruption rhetoric, and a larger role for the armed forces in security and public administration.

He had substantial agenda-setting power through the federal executive, Morena’s legislative coalition, daily presidential conferences, and close military cooperation. His practical authority was limited by federalism, state governments, Congress, courts, autonomous institutions, budgetary and administrative constraints, organized crime, and the need to rely on military and bureaucratic implementers.

The evidence shows strong capacity to mobilize resources and inaugurate or operationalize flagship projects, but mixed implementation quality. The government converted priorities into laws, budgets, military assignments, and infrastructure machinery. However, security outcomes remained poor, social and poverty indicators often lagged the target year, and the Supreme Court invalidated a major electoral reform because of legislative-procedure violations. The record therefore distinguishes political mobilization and visible delivery from durable, independently verified effectiveness.

## 2. Evidence records

### WEB-8B-001 — The government stated and operationalized a broad governing program

The 2023 Fifth Government Report identified the administration’s priorities as social welfare, health, education, infrastructure, energy, public security, and military-supported state capacity. It reported reduced federal crime, expanded National Guard staffing, military involvement in customs and security, construction of Bank of Well-Being branches and hospitals, and military participation in the Maya Train and Isthmus railway.

This is strong evidence of declared priorities and administrative mobilization. It is not sufficient evidence that the reported outcomes were independently verified or that the policies were successful.

- Period: target year and broader ruler-period.
- Attribution: strong for stated goals, executive priorities, and formal assignments.
- Caveat: the source is self-reported and presents favorable government claims.

Source: *Informes de gobierno – Presidente de México*, Presidency of Mexico, 2023. [Source](https://amlo.presidente.gob.mx/informes-de-gobierno/)

### WEB-8B-002 — The administration converted the Maya Train into a formal operating and military-managed structure

In 2023, the government created a formal process for transferring the Maya Train project from FONATUR to the state-owned Tren Maya company, with Sedena personnel coordinating the handover. The project’s transition commission met regularly, established technical, operational, financial, legal, social, and environmental workstreams, and conducted partial handovers of four sections.

The ASF later found that the 2023 handover process itself complied with the relevant lineamientos and detected no irregularities in the audited procedures. This supports effective administrative machinery for transferring and coordinating a flagship project, but does not establish that the project was economically efficient, environmentally sound, or fully operational.

- Period: target year.
- Attribution: strong; the project and military management were presidential priorities.
- Caveat: the audit examined handover procedures, not the project’s full costs, benefits, environmental effects, or long-term ridership.

Sources: ASF, *Informe de Auditoría 2023_0371*, published after the target year, pp. 1–7. [Audit](https://www.asf.gob.mx/Trans/Informes/IR2023b/Documentos/Auditorias/2023_0371_a.pdf); Presidency of Mexico, *Sedena ratifica compromiso de inicio de operaciones del Tren Maya el 15 de diciembre*, 27 November 2023. [Government report](https://amlo.presidente.gob.mx/sedena-ratifica-compromiso-de-inicio-de-operaciones-del-tren-maya-el-15-de-diciembre/)

### WEB-8B-003 — The Maya Train reached a partial operational milestone, while completion remained incomplete

The government stated in November 2023 that the Maya Train would begin service between Campeche and Cancún on 15 December and reported substantial completion of sections, stations, viaducts, and supporting infrastructure. It also acknowledged that large projects could require up to a year to become fully functional.

This shows that the administration achieved a visible short-term implementation milestone, but the government’s own account recognized that inauguration was not equivalent to mature, reliable service. The ASF’s 2023 handover audit recorded incomplete workstreams at year-end, including operational, legal, social, environmental, and property-registration components.

- Period: target year.
- Attribution: strong for the deadline and project-management choice.
- Contrary evidence: the ASF reported only 49.5% progress in the environmental workstream, 77.1% in social matters, and 26% in the federal-property registry workstream at the end of 2023.

Sources: Presidency of Mexico, 27 November 2023. [Government report](https://amlo.presidente.gob.mx/sedena-ratifica-compromiso-de-inicio-de-operaciones-del-tren-maya-el-15-de-diciembre/); ASF, *Informe de Auditoría 2023_0371*, pp. 6–7. [Audit](https://www.asf.gob.mx/Trans/Informes/IR2023b/Documentos/Auditorias/2023_0371_a.pdf)

### WEB-8B-004 — Military mobilization expanded across security, infrastructure, welfare, and administration

The government’s 2023 report stated that the armed forces supported public security, customs, ports, airports, Pemex facilities, Bank of Well-Being branches, hospitals, vaccination, textbook distribution, water infrastructure, railways, and disaster response. The IACHR independently described the continued expansion of the armed forces into civilian administration and public security as institutional backsliding from the planned withdrawal.

This is important evidence of AMLO’s ability to mobilize a powerful national institution across many policy areas. It also shows a major coordination and governance tradeoff: rapid executive implementation increased military influence and reduced the separation between civilian administration and security institutions.

- Period: target year, with major institutional changes inherited from 2019–2022.
- Attribution: strong; military expansion was a central presidential policy.
- Contrary evidence: the government presented military involvement as a way to increase capacity and coverage, while the IACHR raised concerns about civilian control and accountability.

Sources: Presidency of Mexico, 2023 Government Report. [Source](https://amlo.presidente.gob.mx/informes-de-gobierno/); IACHR, *Annual Report 2023, Chapter V: Mexico*, 2024, pp. 3–5 and 104–105. [Report](https://www.oas.org/en/iachr/docs/annual/2023/chapters/IA2023_Cap_5_Mexico_ENG.PDF)

### WEB-8B-005 — The Supreme Court blocked a major presidential electoral reform for procedural defects

On 22 June 2023, the Supreme Court invalidated the second part of López Obrador’s “Plan B” electoral reform by nine votes to two. The Court found multiple serious legislative-procedure violations, including failure to provide legislators adequate access to the text, unjustified urgency, lack of committee deliberation, and improper changes between congressional versions.

This demonstrates both executive ambition and an important implementation failure. The government was able to move the reform through Congress, but it did not convert the proposal into durable law because the process failed constitutional standards and was overturned by an independent court.

- Period: target year.
- Attribution: strong for the presidential program and executive support; legislative responsibility was shared with Morena’s congressional coalition.
- Contrary evidence: the government argued that the reform would reduce electoral costs and privileges; the Court’s ruling addressed procedure rather than every substantive policy objective.

Source: Supreme Court of Mexico, Communiqué No. 222/2023, 22 June 2023. [Decision summary](https://www.internet2.scjn.gob.mx/red2/comunicados/noticia.asp?id=7408)

### WEB-8B-006 — Poverty outcomes improved, but the main evidence measured 2022 rather than 2023

CONEVAL reported in 2023 that poverty had declined to 36.3% in 2022 from 43.9% in 2020, while extreme poverty stood at 7.1%. UNICEF likewise reported that child and adolescent poverty fell to 45.8% in 2022. These are favorable outcomes relative to the pandemic baseline and are consistent with AMLO’s welfare goals.

However, the figures do not directly establish end-2023 performance. They also cannot isolate the effect of presidential programs from wages, remittances, inflation, state programs, and post-pandemic recovery.

- Period: inherited/2022 outcome reported during 2023.
- Attribution: moderate.
- Caveat: the time lag makes this evidence unsuitable as a complete assessment of 2023 implementation.

Sources: CONEVAL, *Medición de la pobreza multidimensional en México 2022*, August 2023. [PDF](https://www.coneval.org.mx/Medicion/MP/Documents/MMP_2022/Pobreza_multidimensional_2022.pdf); UNICEF Mexico, *Informe anual 2023*. [Report page](https://www.unicef.org/mexico/informes/informeanual2023)

### WEB-8B-007 — Security results remained inadequate despite mobilization

INEGI recorded 15,082 homicides in Mexico during January–June 2023, with a preliminary rate of 12 per 100,000 people and firearms involved in 71.3% of cases. The government reported reductions in some federal crimes and homicide over the broader administration, but the independent homicide data show that violent insecurity remained a major national problem.

This is adverse evidence for the effectiveness of the security strategy. It does not establish that federal policy caused every homicide, since criminal violence is highly decentralized and state and municipal authorities retain major responsibilities. It does show that large security mobilization did not produce a safe national environment by mid-2023.

- Period: target year, first half of 2023.
- Attribution: moderate. Federal security policy and the National Guard were important, but criminal violence also depended on state governments, organized crime, local policing, and inherited conditions.
- Contrary evidence: official data showed some decline in federal offenses and certain crime categories.

Source: INEGI, preliminary January–June 2023 homicide statistics, 2023. [Release](https://en.www.inegi.org.mx/app/saladeprensa/noticia/8768)

## 3. Lens dispositions

- **8B.1 — Clear governing program:** Strongly evidenced. The administration repeatedly identified welfare, state-led infrastructure, energy, security, and military-supported capacity as priorities.
- **8B.2 — Turning goals into machinery:** Strongly evidenced. Laws, budgets, military assignments, social programs, and project-management structures translated goals into machinery.
- **8B.3 — Mobilizing the ruling system:** Strongly evidenced. The executive mobilized Morena’s legislative coalition, the armed forces, and federal agencies across multiple sectors.
- **8B.4 — Capable implementers:** Mixed. Military and administrative structures delivered some milestones, but professional autonomy, competence, and appointment quality require more personnel-level research.
- **8B.5 — Coordination and control:** Mixed. The Maya Train transition shows detailed coordination, while health, security, transparency, and military-civilian coordination remained contested.
- **8B.6 — From plans to practice:** Mixed to strong. Major projects and administrative structures became operational, but some were inaugurated before all workstreams and safeguards were complete.
- **8B.7 — Progress toward chosen goals:** Mixed. Poverty and child-poverty measures improved through 2022, while security outcomes remained poor and 2023 evidence is incomplete.
- **8B.8 — Learning and correction:** Incomplete. There is evidence of administrative reorganization and project transition, but insufficient independent evidence that failures systematically produced course correction.
- **8B.9 — Managing crises and resistance:** Mixed. The administration overcame some legislative and administrative obstacles through coalition and military mobilization, but the Supreme Court defeated Plan B and security resistance remained unresolved.
- **8B.10 — Durable goal achievement:** Unresolved/mixed. Some institutions and projects were created or operationalized, but durability, cost-effectiveness, environmental compliance, and post-presidential performance require later evidence.

## 4. Information environment and limitations

- Government reports provide detailed evidence of declared goals, staffing, project milestones, and claimed outcomes, but are self-interested.
- The ASF provides stronger procedural evidence, although audits often examine narrow scopes and may be completed after the target year.
- National poverty statistics released in 2023 largely describe 2022 conditions.
- Security outcomes are affected by federal, state, municipal, and criminal actors, making attribution difficult.
- Military involvement increased executive reach but also reduced transparency and complicated civilian oversight.
- Political polarization encouraged competing narratives: government sources emphasized delivery and reductions, while independent observers emphasized human-rights, security, environmental, and accountability costs.
- A project inauguration or administrative reorganization should not be treated as proof of durable effectiveness.

## 5. Questions for deeper research

1. What were the full 2023 costs, delays, ridership, revenues, environmental effects, and safety performance of the Maya Train?
2. Did the military-led infrastructure and administrative model improve outcomes relative to civilian alternatives?
3. What independent evaluations assessed social transfers, health reform, education, and Banco del Bienestar?
4. Which ministers and agency heads were selected for competence, and which were retained despite implementation failures?
5. What were the full 2023 homicide, disappearance, impunity, and prosecution outcomes?
6. Did the government revise policies after audits, court defeats, or implementation failures?
7. Which 2023 achievements survived the 2024 presidential transition?
8. How much of the poverty decline was attributable to federal policy rather than wages, remittances, and post-pandemic recovery?

## 6. Source groups

### Evidence opened and used

1. Supreme Court of Mexico, Plan B invalidation communiqué.
2. ASF audit of the Maya Train handover process.
3. Presidency of Mexico, 2023 Government Report.
4. Presidency of Mexico, Maya Train operational report, 27 November 2023.
5. IACHR Annual Report 2023, Mexico chapter.
6. INEGI homicide release.
7. CONEVAL 2022 poverty report.
8. UNICEF Mexico annual report.

### Corroborating sources

- El País reporting on the Plan B and institutional resistance.
- Human Rights Watch and IACHR reporting on military expansion and accountability.
- ASF’s broader 2023 audit index: [ASF](https://www.asf.gob.mx/Trans/Informes/IR2023b/index.html)

### Promising leads still requiring inspection

- Full ASF audits of the Maya Train’s construction costs, environmental compliance, procurement, and performance.
- Public accounts and budget-execution data for 2023 flagship projects.
- Independent evaluations of welfare, health, education, and security programs.
- Post-2023 ridership and financial data for the Maya Train.
- Administrative records on appointments, replacement of implementers, and program correction.
- Academic and local research on regional outcomes in southern Mexico.

Source count: 7 developed machine records; 8 principal sources opened or directly examined; several detailed audits and outcome evaluations remain to be inspected. No score assigned.

--- RESEARCH CONTINUATION ROUND 1 ---

I continued the 1B review round and searched the identified gaps directly. The strongest new evidence concerns the March 2023 radioactive-source theft and the existence of operational cyber and biosafety frameworks. I did not find a reliable independent finding of undeclared nuclear activity, a major biological containment escape, or a catastrophic cyber incident attributable to the federal executive in 2023.

I could not update `research-ledger-manifest.json`: the workspace command failed before the repository could be read (`bwrap: loopback: Failed RTM_NEWADDR: Operation not permitted`). The new records below therefore require manifest insertion by the parent workflow. I used new IDs after the existing `WEB-1B-001`–`WEB-1B-007` range.

## New evidence records

### WEB-1B-009 — Four radioactive industrial-radiography sources were stolen and recovered

On 22 March 2023, armed persons entered a licensed facility in Salamanca, Guanajuato, and stole four industrial-radiography devices containing Ir-192 sources. The IAEA event record classified the incident as INES level 1. The devices were recovered on 28 March; the regulator inspected the facility on 29 March and approved improved security measures. The IAEA recorded no public or worker overexposure, no contamination, and no continuing problem, but explicitly recorded degradation of “defence in depth.”

This is the clearest adverse 2023 event for radiological control. It demonstrates a real security breach, but the recovery, inspection, and absence of reported exposure complicate an interpretation of systemic failure. The record does not establish presidential direction or a nationwide control breakdown.

- Period: target year.
- Attribution: weak to moderate. CNSNS and federal civil-protection authorities had regulatory responsibility; no evidence links the incident to a presidential order.
- Source limitation: IAEA event reports are filed and updated by national authorities, which remain responsible for the content.
- Contrary evidence: the sources were recovered within six days, no exposure was reported, and corrective security measures were inspected and approved.

Source: IAEA, *Missing Dangerous Source at Salamanca, Guanajuato, Mexico*, posted 30 March 2023. [Event record](https://www-news.iaea.org/ErfView.aspx?mId=c20a7fa0-2d12-4153-956d-2212c486b76f)

### WEB-1B-010 — Mexico maintained a formal national cyber-incident response protocol

Mexico’s Guardia Nacional CERT-MX published a National Harmonized Cyber-Incident Management Protocol in June 2023. The protocol was intended to coordinate federal agencies, states, autonomous constitutional bodies, academia, and private-sector institutions, with the stated goal of achieving acceptable risk levels and preserving essential governmental and economic functions.

This is evidence of a formal cyber-governance mechanism during the target year. It establishes planning and coordination, not actual resilience. No independent evaluation was found showing that the protocol was fully implemented or materially reduced catastrophic cyber risk.

- Period: target year.
- Attribution: moderate to strong for federal institutional action; practical implementation across states and private operators remains uncertain.
- Source limitation: official description and stated purpose, not an external audit.
- Contrary evidence: independent reporting and professional assessments continued to identify significant ransomware exposure among Mexican public institutions.

Source: Guardia Nacional CERT-MX, *Protocolo Nacional Homologado de Gestión de Incidentes Cibernéticos*, 19 June 2023. [Government source](https://www.gob.mx/gncertmx/articulos/107677)

### WEB-1B-011 — Financial-sector cyber resilience had established response procedures, but incident reporting remained institution-dependent

Banco de México described a cyber-risk strategy based on governance, preventive infrastructure reinforcement, and incident-response teams and protocols. Its framework required financial institutions to detect, report, contain, recover from, and review cyber incidents. The Bank’s website also provides a separate list of significant incidents reported in 2023.

This is favorable evidence for a regulated-sector cyber-resilience framework. It cannot be generalized to all federal systems, biological laboratories, energy infrastructure, or military networks. The Bank notes that incident information is supplied by reporting institutions and may be updated, limiting independent completeness.

- Period: target-year framework and incident reporting.
- Attribution: moderate. Banco de México is constitutionally autonomous, so this is not direct evidence of López Obrador’s personal control; federal financial regulation and interagency coordination remain relevant.
- Source limitation: institutional reporting may omit undisclosed or unreported incidents.
- Contrary evidence: the existence of formal protocols does not demonstrate that all institutions followed them or that attacks caused no material disruption.

Source: Banco de México, *Ciberseguridad, seguridad*, page including the 2023 financial-sector incident listing and response framework. [Source](https://www.banxico.org.mx/sistema-financiero/seguridad-informacion-banco.html)

### WEB-1B-012 — Independent biosafety experts identified active implementation and maintenance risks in Mexican high-containment laboratories

The XV International Symposium on Biosafety and Biosecurity, held in Mexico in August 2023, included sessions on Mexico’s biomedical-waste regulation, translating standards into practice, biosafety programs in universities, geographic distribution of biosafety committees, dual-use risk assessment, high-containment laboratory biosecurity and cybersecurity, laboratory maintenance, and why biocontainment projects fail.

This is independent professional evidence that implementation, maintenance, training, and dual-use governance were recognized as live risks in Mexico’s laboratory system. It does not establish that a specific Mexican laboratory failed, nor that the federal executive caused a particular incident. It is useful adverse-search evidence because it prevents silence from being interpreted as proof of safety.

- Period: target year, with some presentations using earlier data.
- Attribution: weak. The symposium documents sector-wide professional concerns, not presidential conduct.
- Source limitation: conference program rather than peer-reviewed findings or inspection results.
- Contrary evidence: the program also documents active biosafety committees, training, laboratory improvements, and efforts to build institutional frameworks.

Source: AMEXBIO, *XV International Symposium on Biosafety and Biosecurity Program*, 22–25 August 2023, pp. 2–6. [PDF](https://amexbio.org/docs/SIBB23_programa_EN.pdf)

## Gap assessment after continuation

- **Independent nuclear implementation:** Improved for the specific 2023 incident. The IAEA record provides an independent international event framework, although the underlying national report remains authoritative for operational details.
- **Regulatory independence, staffing, maintenance, and emergency preparedness:** Partially improved. The IAEA event record documents regulatory inspection and corrective action; the CNSNS annual-performance report was located but could not be fully fetched in this environment. Its direct inspection, staffing, and emergency-preparedness findings remain to be extracted.
- **Adverse biological-security evidence:** Partially improved. The AMEXBIO program shows independent professional concern about high-containment security, cybersecurity, maintenance, and dual-use risk, but not a confirmed breach or failure.
- **Adverse cybersecurity evidence:** Partially improved. Mexico had a national incident protocol and financial-sector response structures, while public reporting indicates continuing ransomware exposure. A definitive independent 2023 assessment of federal critical-infrastructure resilience was not found.
- **Radioactive-source theft:** Resolved as a documented 2023 event. It should not be treated as proof of a nationwide control failure without additional incident-rate, inspection, or regulator-performance evidence.
- **Undeclared nuclear activity:** No credible new evidence found. This remains an unanswered question, not a favorable inference from silence.
- **Presidential attribution:** Remains high-risk. The new records mainly establish federal or sectoral institutions and events, not personal presidential decisions.

No score or directional chapter judgment is assigned.
